# LogVOLE: Succinct and Efficient Chosen-Input VOLE for ZK and Beyond

### Lucien K. L. Ng

∗

### Peter Rindal

†

### Akash Shah

‡

Abstract Random Vector Oblivious Linear Evaluation (VOLE) correlations are a widely used backend for zero- knowledge proofs and secure computation, and can now be generated with strong concrete efficiency. Many applications, however, need correlations on the receiver’s actual input. They therefore start from random VOLE and send a linear-size derandomization vector, which remains a main communication bottleneck. We study chosen-input VOLE (CI-VOLE), where the receiver privately chooses a large vectorx, the sender fixes ∆, and the parties obtain shares ofx*·*∆ without communicating a linear-size object. This work presentsLogVole, a concretely efficientCI-VOLEprotocol with polylogarithmic end-to-end communi- cation under Ring-LWE. The construction uses a recursive shrink/expand design: it authenticates short digests of the chosen input and then uses a succinct telescope to expand those relations back to the full vector. The protocol has*O*(*λ*log²(*|*x*|*+*m*msg)) one-time setup and query communication and*O*(*|*x*|/n*) ring operations. Here,*n*is the underlying ring degree,*λ*is the computational security parameter, and *m*msg is the size of the shares. LogVolealso supports a public-key non-interactive mode: for a fixed ∆, the sender publishes reusable parameters, and a receiver sends one compactx-dependent message to obtain the matching VOLE shares. This gives a route to non-interactive VOLE-based ZK with polylogarithmic communication for arbitrary circuits. We also give a malicious-security extension in the random oracle model. UsingLogVole, we obtain the first concretely efficient VOLE-based ZK protocol with polylogarithmic communication for arbitrary circuits. At 128-bit computational and 40-bit statistical security, our imple- mentation reaches 12*.*9 millionZ*p* inputs/s, 9*.*0 million ZK multiplication gates/s on a 16-core machine, and proves 1024*×*1024 matrix multiplication in about 4s with single-thread computation and 226 KB communication.

## 1 Introduction

### 1.1 Motivation and Overview

Vector Oblivious Linear Evaluation (VOLE) correlations are a central backend in modern zero-knowledge proofs, secure computation, PSI, and authenticated computation. In a VOLE instance, the sender holds a global key ∆, the receiver holds a vectorx, and the parties obtain additive shares ofx*·*∆, where the receiver’s share is also an authenticated label forx. Random VOLE is now a major success story: PCG and silent- correlation techniques generate random VOLE, or closely related correlations, with sublinear communication and strong concrete efficiency [BCGI18, BCG + 19b, SGRR19, BCG + 19a, YWL + 20, CRR21, RRT23]. Chosen-input VOLE (CI-VOLE), which is the main goal of this work, asks for a stronger interface. The receiver chooses a private length-*w*vectorx, the sender fixes ∆, and the parties should obtain a length- *w*sharing ofx*·*∆. Thus both the chosen input and the output correlation are linear-size objects, while the desired communication is sublinear. From the viewpoint of mainstream MPC, this is not a primitive one expects to obtain by a generic transformation. OT- or Beaver-triple-based approaches normally touch the receiver’s chosen input entry by entry. A naive homomorphic-encryption approach can let the receiver compute encrypted functions of a short seed or digest, but it still leaves the problem of turning the encrypted ∗ luciengkl@gatech.edu † peterrindal@gmail.com ‡ akashshah08@g.ucla.edu

result into sender/receiver VOLE shares without revealingxor communicating a linear-size object. This is not a lower bound; it is the practical intuition that makes the functionality remarkable. PriorCI-VOLEconstructions show that this intuition is not a barrier in principle. They prove that sublinear, and even logarithmic, communication is possible [ARS24, AMR25]. That theoretical possibility is already striking; the remaining question is whether it can become a concrete primitive with small end-to-end communication, SIMD-friendly computation, and an API that fits real applications. Concrete efficiency matters because VOLE is a backend, not an isolated goal. In VOLE-based ZK, random VOLE gives fast authenticated shares for a random vector, but the prover must still derandomize the random correlation onto the actual execution trace by sending a linear-size correction vector. The same pattern appears in other applications where random correlations are easy but correlations on a later chosen private input are the real object. A succinctCI-VOLEprimitive removes this linear derandomization step rather than optimizing around it. The closest concrete precursor isTinyLabel[DLL25], which introduced a powerful shrink/expand pattern for authenticated input-label delivery. TinyLabels compresses a long input to a short digest, derives a short digest-dependent key, and uses structured ciphertexts to expand that key back to selected labels. This is a remarkable concrete kernel, and it is the starting point of our construction. However, TinyLabels does not by itself give (private staged)CI-VOLEwith logarithmic end-to-end communication: its target functionality is delivering garbled input labels with the inputs revealed in the clear, and the reusable material remains linear in the expanded size. LogVolerealizes the full potential of this shrink/expand idea for chosen-input VOLE. We view the TinyLabels expansion as an algebraic telescope: a short authenticated relation on a*digestd*x*i.e.*, a compact x-dependent message, can be translated back into authenticated relations on all entries ofx. We make this telescoping process succinct by leveraging its reusability and self-similarity. Each recursive level replaces a block of chosen inputs by a short digest vector, obtains authenticated shares for that digest vector from a smaller instance, and then expands those shares back to the original block. The result is a concretely efficient CI-VOLEprotocol with polylogarithmic end-to-end communication under the Ring-LWE assumption. This structure also gives flexible ordering for when the inputs are chosen and when the shares are released. In the*staged*mode, the sender can obtain its share before the receiver selectsx, which is the ordering needed by garbling-style applications. In the public-key non-interactive mode, the sender publishes reusable parameters for a fixed ∆; later, any receiver with a chosenxderives its VOLE share locally and sends the digest to let the sender recover the corresponding share. Prior works also show the feasibility of such non-interactive interfaces, butLogVolegives this public-key mode together with the concrete efficiency and polylogarithmic end-to-end cost needed for applications such as non-interactive VOLE-based ZK. In this paper, we demonstrate the strong concrete performance of our semi-honest interactiveLogVole. We applyLogVoleto ZK applications, where we show strong concrete efficiency by combining semi-honest CI-VOLEwith the standard ZKP techniques of witness binding and IT-MAC checks. Our computational performance is comparable to prior work on (prover-efficient) IT-MAC-based ZK, while our communication drops from linear to polylogarithmic in the verification circuit size. For garbled-input compression, we show concrete efficiency with substantial communication savings over prior work. Finally, we propose various extensions toward malicious security. In terms of functionality, Figure 1 states the basic semi-honestCI-VOLEfunctionality. The generalized functionality with flexible release orderings is given later in Section 8.

### 1.2 Our Contributions

•We constructLogVole, the first concretely efficientCI-VOLEprotocol we know of that combines private chosen inputs, polylogarithmic end-to-end communication, and SIMD-friendly Ring-LWE computation in the practical parameter regime needed for (prover-efficient) IT-MAC-based ZK and garbling-style appli- cations. More precisely, the parties can generateCI-VOLEshares for length-*w*inputs and outputs over a message space of size*m*with*O*(*λ·*(log*w*+*m*) 2 ) communication for both one-time setup and each query. •We propose two versions ofCI-VOLE: staged and public-key non-interactive. In the staged version, the sender’s shares can be obtained before the receiver choosesx, which is needed for garbled input compres- sion. In the public-key non-interactive version, the sender publishes reusable parameters for a fixed ∆; later, any receiver with a chosenxderives its VOLE share locally and sends a*digest*to the sender, enabling

This non-interactive interface is highly desirable for,*e.g.*, non-interactive VOLE-based ZK. •UsingLogVole, we obtain the first concretely efficient IT-MAC-based ZK protocol we know of with poly- logarithmic communication for arbitrary circuits. •We obtain garbled-input compression and authenticated input-label delivery with logarithmic one-time setup and compact per-query communication. •We propose various extensions toward malicious security for ourCI-VOLE. This is challenging due to the sublinear communication. We investigate the security of the digest scheme used inLogVoleand argue for extractability of the receiver’s input. We also combine these ideas with various consistency-check techniques to obtain malicious security against a corrupt sender. •We implementLogVoleand evaluate it in VOLE-based ZK and garbled input compression, showing that the concrete communication savings can coexist with high computational throughput.

### 1.3 Roadmap

The main body follows the construction before its applications. Sections 2 and 3 position the work and set up the security, lattice, and encoding tools. Section 4 gives the high-level shrink/expand idea, Section 5 isolates the core recursive mechanism for better explanation, and Sections 6 and 7 describe the lower-level realization and the optimizations needed for concrete efficiency. Sections 8 and 9 state the generalizedCI-VOLEinterface and the full construction. The main body then discusses the malicious-security extension, ZK application, and implementation results in Sections 10 to 12. The supplementary material gives the digest analysis, correctness and semi-honest security proofs, truncation analysis, malicious-security proofs, the opened-setup ZK proof, and the detailed comparisons and application numbers.

## 2 Related Work

Random VOLE.A long line of work [BCGI18, BCG + 19b, SGRR19, BCG + 19a, YWL + 20, BCG + 20, CRR21, BCG + 22, RRT23, BBC + 24, KPRR25, LXYY25, KPR + 25] shows how to generate random VOLE correlations, or closely related silent correlations, with sublinear communication and strong concrete effi- ciency. [BCG + 19a] gives a concretely efficient two-round silent-correlation framework. A related public-key PCF line makes the silent-correlation interface non-interactive: after parties publish public keys, they can derive random OT/VOLE-style correlations without further interaction [OSY21, BCM + 24, CDH + 25]. Taken together, the high efficiency of these works makes random correlations essentially “solved” in practice when the receiver can accept a random vector in advance. However, they do not directly address the chosen-input setting that we need: once the receiver later wants shares of a specific vectorx, prior VOLE-based applica- tions still need a linear-size derandomization step to convert random VOLE into shares ofx*·*∆. Our work is complementary to this line. Rather than further optimizing random correlations, we target the missing func- tionality and realize private chosen-input correlations directly, thereby removing the linear derandomization

This is conceptually important because it eliminates the derandomization step used in prior VOLE-based protocols, but the resulting con- structions are computationally expensive; for example, the*O*(*|*x*|* 1*/*2 ) regime incurs*O*(*|*x*|* 3*/*2 ) computation. Staged CI-VOLE.If one strengthens CI-VOLE so that the sender must learn its share before the receiver’s input is fixed, then the closest prior line is not phrased as VOLE but as authenticated input-label delivery for garbled circuits [AIKW13, DLL25]. In these works, the garbler fixes the 0-labels (the labels representing wire value 0) and the global offset ∆ during the one-time setup, and later the evaluator recovers labels

of the form*Kxi*=*K₀* +*xi·*∆. This is the closest prior analogue to the “commitment-style” aspect of ourCI-VOLEfunctionality, namely that the sender-side masks are available before the actual input is chosen. TinyLabels [DLL25] is the main practical precursor: it gives a concretely efficient shrink-and-expand mechanism based on laconic encryption and linearly homomorphic encryption, but in a garbled-circuit setting where the input vectorxis effectively*public*to the sender, the one-time setup remains*O*(*|*x*|λ*+poly(*λ*)), and rate 1 +*o*(1) is reached only after amortizing this setup across many instances. Our protocol lifts this viewpoint to CI-VOLE:LogVolecan hide the receiver’s input in the staged functionality while reducing one-time setup and per-query communication costs to*O*(*λ*log² *|*x*|*) bits. Table 1 summarizes the landscape across random VOLE, priorCI-VOLE, staged predecessors, and our construction. IT-MAC-based ZKP.A sequence of works [DIO21a, YSWW21, DILO22, LHLA25] shows that VOLE- generated information-theoretic MACs yield constant-round, fast, and scalable zero-knowledge proofs with low prover overhead; QuickSilver and its successors [YSWW21, DILO22, LHLA25] are the main examples. These systems still begin with random VOLE correlations and must derandomize them to the actual witness by sending a correction vector, incurring communication linear in the trace size. AntMan [WYY + 22] reduces this cost by combining VOLE with additively homomorphic encryption, achieving sublinear communication, but its communication is still*O*(*|*x*|* 3*/*4 ), and it did not demonstrate concrete efficiency experimentally. In contrast, our work replaces the random-VOLE generation step itself with a concretely efficientCI-VOLE protocol, so the standard IT-MAC / QuickSilver-style checking layer can be retained while the end-to-end communication drops to*O*(*λ*log²(*|*x*|*+*σ*)) for arbitrary circuits. the evaluator’s input labels can be transferred with query communication*|*x*|*+poly(*λ*) under assumptions such as RSA, LWE, or DDH, but only at the cost of Ω(*|*x*|λ*) one-time setup communication. Follow-up works [GS18, GOS18] obtained comparable query guarantees under weaker assumptions such as Factoring or CDH, though via non-black-box techniques that are prohibitively expensive in practice. [ABI + 23] later achieved overall rate 1 +*o*(1) using stronger tools such as RSA, bilinear DDH, or iO with SSBH, but these constructions remain far from concretely efficient. The closest prior work to ours is TinyLabels [DLL25], which is the first concretely efficient Ring-LWE-based scheme in this line. Yet it suffers from the drawbacks listed in the staged CI-VOLE section above: an expensive one-time setup and leakage of the input vector to the sender.

### 3.2 Detailed Description of Security Model

A staticsemi-honestadversary*A*corrupts one of the two parties participating in the 2PC protocol at the outset and attempts to infer as much information as possible from the protocol execution while honestly following its specifications. In the context ofCI-VOLE(Figure 1), security is defined in the real/ideal simulation paradigm in the random oracle model. The protocol uses the random oracle in the usual way to derive pseudorandom ciphertext components and Fiat–Shamir challenges. Separately, Section A analyzes the concrete laconic digest itself and isolates the smudging, hiding, and binding properties needed in the security proofs. In the real interaction, the parties execute the protocol Π in the presence of*A*and the environment*Z*. We writeREALΠ*,A,Z*to denote the binary distribution ensemble describing*Z*’s output in the real interaction. In the ideal execution, the parties send their inputs to an ideal functionality*F*. We denote the adversary in the ideal world bySim. We writeIDEAL*F,S,Z*to denote the binary distribution ensemble describing*Z*’s output in the ideal interaction. A protocol Π securely realizes a functionality*F*if for every adversary*A*in the real interaction, there exists an adversary*S*in the ideal interaction, such that no environment*Z*can tell apart the real and ideal interactions, except with negligible probability. A staticmaliciousadversary*A*corrupts one of the two parties participating in the 2PC protocol at the outset and may deviate arbitrarily from the protocol in an attempt to learn information or force outputs beyond what is allowed by the ideal functionality. One challenge in the malicious proof is that the simulator is not handed the corrupted party’s effective input; it must recover that input from the adversarial transcript before invoking the ideal functionality. We defer malicious security to the dedicated sections. Section 10 states the malicious protocol-level changes, and Section 3.8 records the NIZK interface used there. Sections E.1 to E.3 set up the proof roadmap and transcript notation. Sections E.4, E.4.1 and 10.1.6 give the receiver-extraction interfaces used by the malicious proof. Sections E.5 and E.6 prove the corrupted-receiver and corrupted-sender cases, respectively, and Section E.7 combines them into the full malicious CI-VOLE theorem of Theorem 14.

### 3.5 Linearly Homomorphic Encryption

We now record the full formal interface of Linearly Homomorphic Encryption. The reason the interface has two encryption algorithms is that the target decryption value is an*affine*expression

*m₁ ·y*+*m₂.*

Accordingly,LHE*.*Enc₁ encrypts the coefficient vector*m₁* that will later be scaled by the public query*y*, whileLHE*.*Enc₂ encrypts the offset vector*m₂* that is carried through additively. The role ofLHE*.*KeyGenis then to combine the two hidden states with the chosen*y*so that decryption returns this affine combination and not the two terms separately.

Definition 7(Noisy Linearly Homomorphic Encryption over Rings (LHE)).*An*LHE*scheme overRq*(*λ*) *with error boundB*LHE(*λ*)*comprises the following algorithms:* $ *λ w* *•*pp *←−*LHE*.*Setup(1*,*1)*: The algorithm takesλand input vector dimensionwas input, and outputs public* *parameters*pp*. All the remaining algorithms of*LHE*implicitly take*pp*as input.* $ *w* *•*(ct₁*,*st₁) *←−*LHE*.*Enc₁(*m₁*)*: On input vectorm₁ ∈Rq, the algorithm outputs ciphertext*ct₁ *and a state* st₁ *for the term later multiplied byy.* $ *w* *•*(ct₂*,*st₂) *←−*LHE*.*Enc₂(*m₂*)*: On input vectorm₂ ∈Rq, the algorithm outputs ciphertext*ct₂ *and a state* st₂ *for the additive offset term.* *•*sk*y←*LHE*.*KeyGen(st₁*,*st₂*,y*)*: On input the two statesst₁ andst₂ output byEnc₁ andEnc₂, respectively,* *and a ring elementy∈Rq, the algorithm outputs a decryption keysky.* *•m*res*←*LHE*.*Dec(sk*y,*ct₁*,*ct₂*,y*)*: The algorithm takes decryption key*sk*y, the two ciphertexts*ct₁*,*ct₂*, and* *ring elementy. It outputs a decrypted messagem*res*∈R* *w*

*q.*

The full formal statement appears in Appendix 3.6.3.* *•*Security (Informal).*The receiver’s view consisting of ciphertexts and functional decryption keys can be*

(*t*) (*t*) (*t*)

### 3.7 Zero-Knowledge Arguments

We recall the standard definition of zero-knowledge arguments from the literature [GMW87, Kil92, Gol01, IKOS07] below. An NP relation*R*(*x,w*) is an efficiently decidable binary relation which is polynomially bounded, meaning that there exists a polynomial*p*(*·*) such that*R*(*x,w*) is satisfied then*|w|≤p*(*|x|*). We view*R*(*x,·*) as an arithmetic circuit*Cx*comprising addition and multiplication gates. For*x*in the NP- language,*Cx*on a valid witness outputs 0, i.e.,*Cx*(*w*) = 0 indicates*R*(*x,w*) = 1.

A zero-knowledge argument system for an NP relation*R*(*x,w*) is defined by two probabilistic polynomial- time (PPT) algorithms, a proverPand a verifierV. BothPandVhold the common input statement*x*, whilePadditionally holds a witness*w*. BothPandVinteract using the next-message function defined by their respective protocols. The next- message function takes the inputs, the messages received so far from the other party, and the randomness, and outputs the next message to be sent to the other party.

Definition 8(Zero-Knowledge Arguments). *The protocol*(P(*x,w*)*,*V(*x*))*is a zero-knowledge argument for an NP relationRif it satisfies the following:* *•Perfect Completeness.If a statement is true, an honest prover*P*can always convince an honest verifier*

V*. For any pair*(*x,w*)*such thatR*(*x,w*) = 1*(meaningx∈L),*V*accepts the proof.*
#### Pr [(P(x,w),V(x)) =accept] = 1.

*•Computational Soundness.A malicious PPT prover*P *∗* *cannot convince an honest verifier*V*of a* *false statement except with negligible probability. Formally, for every PPT corrupt prover*P *∗* *, there exists* *a negligible function*negl(*·*)*such that for anyx /∈L(i.e.*∄*wsuch thatR*(*x,w*) = 1*),*

Pr [(P *∗* *,*V)(*x*) =*accept*]*≤*negl(*λ*)*.*

*•Zero-Knowledge.A malicious verifier*V *∗* *learns nothing from the interaction other than the fact that the* *statement is true. Formally, for every PPT corrupt verifier*V *∗* *, there exists a PPT simulator*SimV*∗ that* *takes as input a statementx∈Land outputs a transcript that is computationally indistinguishable from* *the view of*V *∗* *in a real interaction between*P*and*V*. That is, for all pairs*(*x,w*)*such thatR*(*x,w*) = 1*,*

*{view*V*∗* (P(*x,w*)*,*V *∗*

(*x*))*}≈c{*SimV*∗* (*x*)*},*
*where view*V*∗ denotes the verifier’s complete view of the interaction (all received messages and its inter-* *nal random coin tosses) and the symbol≈cdenotes computational indistinguishability between the two* *probability ensembles.*

Remark.A protocol (P(*x,w*)*,*V(*x*)) is an*honest-verifier*zero-knowledge argument system for an NP relation*R*if it satisfies perfect completeness and the computational soundness property as defined in Defi- nition 8. However, zero-knowledge simulability is required only with respect to an honest verifier. Formally, there exists a PPT simulatorSimsuch that for all pairs (*x,w*) such that*R*(*x,w*) = 1,

*{*view*V*(P(*x,w*)*,*V(*x*))*}≈c{*Sim(*x*)*}.*

### 3.8 Non-Interactive Arguments of Knowledge

In the malicious protocol we use Fiat–Shamir non-interactive arguments of knowledge for bounded linear re- lations over*Rq*, instantiated by the lattice proof systems of Bootle–Lyubashevsky–Seiler and Lyubashevsky– Nguyen–Plan¸con [BLS19, LNP22]. We use the following standard random-oracle interface. Let*R*(*x,w*) be an efficiently decidable relation. A Fiat–Shamir NIZK argument of knowledge for*R* consists of PPT algorithms ZK*.*Setup*,*ZK*.*Prove *H* *,*ZK*.*Verify *H* *,*

together with a simulatorZK*.*Sim *H* and an extractorZK*.*Ext *H*. Setup outputs public proof parametersppzk. The prover, given (*x,w*) with*R*(*x,w*) = 1, outputs*π←*ZK*.*Prove *H* (ppzk*,x,w*). The verifier outputs a bitZK*.*Verify *H* (ppzk*,x,π*). We require the following properties, for polynomially many statements in one random-oracle execution. •Completeness.For every (*x,w*) with*R*(*x,w*) = 1, an honestly generated proof verifies except with negligible probability.

•Zero knowledge / simulated proofs.There is a PPT simulatorZK*.*Sim *H*, with the usual ability to program the Fiat–Shamir random-oracle challenge points, that on input a public statement*x*outputs an accepting simulated proof. For true statements, simulated proofs are computationally indistinguishable from honest proofs to any malicious verifier. We will also invoke this simulator on public statements generated by other simulators; in that use, we only rely on acceptance and on the fact that the simulated proof is produced without a witness. •Knowledge soundness.For every PPT prover*P* *⋆*, if*P* *⋆* produces an accepting proof for*x*with non- negligible probability, then the extractorZK*.*Ext *H*, with rewindable access to*P* *⋆* and the same public parameters, outputs*w*such that*R*(*x,w*) = 1, except with negligible extraction error. For the setup proofs in Section 10.1.3,*R*is the bounded exact modular linear relation certified by the corresponding public matrix, target, and coefficient bounds.

## 4 High-Level Technical Overview

The goal ofLogVoleis to efficiently distributeCI-VOLEcorrelated randomness. The starting point of our construction isTinyLabel[DLL25], a specialized protocol for GC input-label delivery with succinct commu- nication. We provide an overview ofTinyLabelin Section 4.1. Then, in Section 4.2, we use it as a building block to constructLogVole, our polylogarithmic-communication CI-VOLE scheme. This overview is mostly for our*staged*construction, except that in Section 4.4, we present the non-interactive variant. Finally, we describe its applications in detail in Section 4.6.

### 4.1 Revisiting TinyLabels

We useTinyLabelas the conceptual starting point for our construction. For our purposes, however, it is more convenient to present it in a simplified form. What we want the reader to keep in mind.The mental model is the following.Sholds a global secret ∆*∈M*and wantsRto obtain a vector

m= ∆*·*x+k*∈M* *w* *,*

wherex*∈M* *w* is chosen byRandk*∈M* *w* is a mask known toS. We call such an affine equation a*relation*: Sholds the maskk, andRholds the valuemsatisfying the displayed equation. This is the CI-VOLE shape we ultimately care about. VOLE can also be thought of as a secret sharing ofx*∈M* *w* times ∆*∈M*, where m*,*k*∈M* *w* are the shares. We will sometimes denote this secret sharing ofxtimes ∆ as*⟨*x*·*∆*⟩*, wherem*,*k are implicit. This is the basic CI-VOLE functionality captured in Figure 1. TinyLabels does*not*quite solve this problem in our intended private-input setting. Instead, in the garbled-circuit setting that motivates TinyLabels, the evaluator’s inputxis already handled in a somewhat public way: the garbler effectively knows the masked input vectorxand may send it in the clear. So the object being transferred is better viewed as a*semi-public linear correlation*than as a private VOLE correlation. For understanding our construction, that distinction is important: TinyLabels gives a semi-succinct way to deliver m= ∆*·*x+k*,*

but in a setting wherexis not hidden fromS. The laconic starting point.The key technical ingredient behind TinyLabels is laconic encryption[DKL + 23a]. At a moral level, it givesSa way to encrypt a vector of repeated secrets and later lets anyone locally combine that ciphertext with an input vectorx. The result looks like a masked linear relation:

LacE*.*Eval(ctlac*,*x)*≈*r*·d*x*−*∆*·*x*,*

where •ctlac=LacE*.*Enc(∆;r) is a ciphertext prepared byS, •r*∈M* *w* is encryption randomness, and •*d*x=LacE*.*Digest(x) is a*short*digest ofx.

So laconic encryption already has a clear VOLE-like flavor: it almost gives a sharing of ∆*·*x, but with an annoying mask termr*·d*xattached to it. This is the central point. The core obstacle.That mask term depends on*d*x, hence on the eventual runtime inputx. Therefore, laconic encryption by itself does not yet giveSa*fixed*maskkknown in advance. But in garbled circuits, Sneeds exactly such a fixed mask ahead of time, because those values are part of the garbling material. So the problem is:

How can we replace thex-dependent maskr*·d*xby a maskkthat is fixed beforexis chosen?

TinyLabels’ answer: a short key for the correction term.TinyLabels resolves this by adding a linearly homomorphic encryption layer. Here is the simplified TinyLabels flow in dependency order. For this overview, we keep the abstract spaces already used above: 2

In this overview, we identify these dimensions and write both as*w*in order to keep the dependency flow readable.

Why this is succinct.The key asymmetry is between the large offline objects and the short online object. S’s offline material—namely the laconic ciphertextctlacand the LHE ciphertextsctr*,*ctk—still has size linear in*w*, since it encrypts vectors of length*w*. What becomes succinct is thex*-dependent*part. Oncexis fixed and public,Sdoes not send a length-*w*correction vector. Instead, it sends only the short derived key

skx=skr*·d*x+skk*,*

which depends onxonly through the short digest*d*x. Thus the online dependence onxhas been compressed from a vector-sized object to a single digest-sized key. What matters for us.ForLogVole, the important takeaway is not the exact garbled-circuit packaging, but the structural pattern underneath it. TinyLabels first*shrinks*R’s long input vectorxto a short digest *d*x, and then uses a short key derived from*d*xto*expand*back to the desired linear correlation (∆*·*x+k). This shrink/expand viewpoint is the real conceptual bridge to our construction. It isolates the part that depends on the full vectorxinto a short digest, and postpones the recovery of the full label vector to a later local computation. What TinyLabels does*not*give us.From the CI-VOLE viewpoint, TinyLabels still falls short in two ways. First, it does not protect privacy ofR’s input: in TinyLabels’ target use case,xis effectively available toS, so the resulting correlation is better viewed as semi-public than as private chosen-input VOLE. Second, although the online response is short, the offline setup remains linear in*|*x*|*, becauseSmust still prepare and transmit vector-sized ciphertexts such asctlac,ctr, andctk. These are exactly the two limitations that our construction addresses next: we hidexfromS, and we compress the offline cost from linear to polylogarithmic.

### 4.2 Overview of LogVole

Our constructionLogVoledrastically reduces the (one-time) setup and online communication cost to*O*(log*|Rq|*), where*Rq*is the ring used by the underlying RLWE ciphertexts. In this section, we explain the structural moves that make this possible. From now on, we work in the private-input setting:Rknowsxand must not reveal it toS. Step 1: remove the public-x assumption.The first obstacle is that TinyLabels, as stated, assumes the digest input is effectively public. In particular, if one writes

skx=skr*·d*x+skkwhere*d*x=Digest(x)*,*

then any party that computesskxfromskr*,*skkand*d*xmust learn the digest inputxitself, or at least some information about it. In particular, TinyLabel only used the digest in a functional way that authenticated *x*but did not hide it. This is incompatible with CI-VOLE, whereRmust keepxhidden fromS. Our approach leans on the fact that*d*x=Digest(*x*) is essentially the root of a Merkle tree. We prove that for this particular instantiation ofDigest, if one randomizes the input then the digest is hiding. Concretely, Rsamples fresh randomness*ζ*and computes

*d*x:=Digest(x;*ζ*)*.*

The protocol then proceeds exactly as before, except that all digest-dependent quantities are defined with respect to this randomized digest value, a commitment tox. The key point is that the randomness*ζ*raises the entropy of the digest input, and our later analysis shows thatDigestis hiding on such inputs. Thus,*d*x=Digest(x;*ζ*) can be revealed and used in the TinyLabels derivationskx=skr*·d*x+skkwithout exposingxtoS. We detail the digest randomization in Section 6.4. With randomization, the digest serves not only as a compression mechanism but also as a privacy- preserving handle. Step 2: recall the TinyLabels ping-pong in the form we will reuse.the main communication bottle- neck inherited from TinyLabels is the linear-size (*|*x*|*) offline setup:Smust prepare vector-sized ciphertexts, in particular

ctlac=LacE*.*Enc((∆*,...,*∆);r)*,* ctr=LHE*.*Enc(skr*,*r)*,* ctk=LHE*.*Enc(skk*,*k)*.*

Before we compress this setup, it is worth pausing and restating the TinyLabels flow in the form we will reuse repeatedly. The interaction has a very simple “ping-pong” structure:

1.Rsends the short digest*d*x=Digest(x) over its chosenx.
2.Sreplies with the short keyskx=skr*·d*x+skk.
3.Usingskxand Equation (2),Rturns this*digest-side relation*into the corresponding*output-side relation*
cx=r*·d*x+k*.*

It then uses the same cancellation as in Equation (3) to obtain

#### cx−LacE.Eval(ctlac,x) = ∆·x+k.

Step 3: separate reusable setup from fresh correction.Now we can make the next structural observation. Among the large offline objects above, the pair

(ctlac*,*ctr)

depends only on the fixed secret ∆ and the associated reusable randomnessr, as defined in Equation (1). This part can therefore be viewed as a*reusable setup*. By contrast, the correction side must be fresh for each invocation:Ssamples a fresh mask vectork, a fresh offset keyskk, and forms the corresponding correction ciphertextctk. To avoid the linear-size communication due toctk,TinyLabelfound a way to avoid sending it: In the random-oracle view, one can equivalently think of this fresh correction ciphertext as being sampled pseudorandomly, for example as ctk=*H*(seed)

withSusingskkto recover the associated maskk=LHE*.*Dec(skk*,*ctk). (There are subtleties about the validity of the random ciphertext. We delay the discussion to Section 7.2.) So once ∆ is fixed, the ∆-dependent material can be cached and reused across many choices ofx. What changes from instance to instance is only the fresh*d*x*,*ctk. These two displays should be read in that order: first the digest-side relation, then the output-side relation. Equation (4) is the batched*digest-side relation*for the TinyLabels-style expansion step: it is the short relation on the digests. Equation (5) is the batched*output-side relation*: it is the full relation on the original blocks. They have the same form. Only the ambient objects have changed: the global key ∆ on the output side is replaced by the reusable keyskron the digest side, and the long vectorsx*i*are replaced by their short digests*d*x*i*. p Next we show the power of the reuse idea alone to obtain *|*x*|*communication. After that, we also leverage this self-similarity of Equation (4), (5) and recurse, which drives the communication down to logarithmic.

Warm-up: the balancing trick.Once the reusable setup is separated from the fresh correction side, a simple amortization tradeoff becomes visible. The basic approach with*µ*= 1 has linear offline com- munication, coming from the reusable setup, and constant online communication, coming from the short digest-dependent messages*d*x*,*skx. If one prepares reusable setup for*blocks*of size*µ*and applies it across *|*x*|/µ*blocks, then the total communication is

*Oλ*(*µ*+*|*x*|/µ*)*.* p p Choosing*µ*= Θ( *|*x*|*) balances the two terms and gives total communication*Oλ*( *|*x*|*). This warm-up is not yet the main result. Its role is to isolate the structural lesson: once the reusable setup is separated from the fresh correction side, one can trade setup size against the number of invocations. 3

The real gain comes next. Recursion.Now we introduce the high-level idea in ourLogVolethat drives the setup cost down to logarithmic. The key observation is that the batched digest-side relation in (4) is itself in the same CI-VOLE shape as the batched output-side relation in (5), but over a smaller domain. It is helpful to isolate this as a shrink/expand picture. The expansion step starts from a digest-side relation, i.e. Equation (4), and returns an output-side relation on the underlying blocks, i.e. Equation (5). This suggests recursion. We can generate the digest-side relation as the output-side relation of an even smaller instance. If we repeat this operation recursively we obtain Shrink Shrink Shrink (x*ℓ*) x *−−−→d*x*−−−→ ··· −−−→d* Expand Expand Expand (x*ℓ*) () *⟨*x*·*∆*⟩ ←−−−−⟨d*x*·*sk*r⟩ ←−−−− ··· ←−−−−⟨d ·*sk*rℓ⟩*

The recursive idea is to avoid generating the digest-side relation in Equation (4) directly by a fresh linear-size setup. Instead, we produce it by running the same kind of mechanism again, but now on the digest vector

dx= (*d*x1*,...,d*x*µ*)*.*

is the input to another shrink/expand instance with smaller size. Concretely, the vector skx= (skx1*,...,*skx*µ*) already has exactly the algebraic form of a CI-VOLE output: it is a global secret times a message vector, plus a mask vector. The only difference is that the message vector is now the shorter digest vector rather than the original blocks. So the output of one level naturally serves as the input of the next. This is the master recursion step. At the top level, we want authenticated outputs for the original blocks
x₁*,...,*x*µ*. To obtain them, it suffices to have authenticated inputs for the digests*d*x1*,...,d*x*µ*. But those
authenticated digest inputs can themselves be produced by the same construction, after grouping the digests into smaller blocks. Repeating this process forms a tree of shrinking instances. Each level reduces the description length, while the reusable setup at that level is amortized across many children. After*O*(log*|*x*|*) levels, the remaining instance is constant size, so the total setup cost becomes logarithmic. p This is why the earlier input/output parallelism matters. The reuse idea alone gives the *|*x*|*warm-up. The additional observation that the input layer and output layer have the same algebraic form is what makes full recursion possible. Section 5 opens this shrink/expand step and explains the local telescope that makes the expansion possible. Further reusing the setup material.Above, we described the recursion in a way that treats each level as a separate shrink/expand instance with different keysk

|||. Thus,Sshould sendO(log₂ |x|) sets of (ct|,ct )|

for the*O*(log₂ *|*x*|*) levels. However, observe that by reusing the sameskracross all non-base levels*i.e.*,

sk =sk =*···*=sk*,*

Sonly needs to send one set of (ct*,*ct) for the entire recursion, thus saving a factor of*O*(log₂ *|*x*|*) in the setup cost. Yet, it does not entirely eliminate the logarithmic factor in communication. It is due to our optimization in Section 7.2, which reduces the ring element size from*O*(*λ³*) to*O*(*λ*log² *|*x*|*). While it reintroduces <u>dependence on log|x|</u>in communication, it actually improves the performance since log*|*x*|≪λ*.

In fact,TinyLabelalready remarked this tradeoff to attain*Oλ*(p*|*x*|*) setup cost. However, they did not exploit the self- similarity between the input and output layers to recurse, so they stopped at this warm-up.

### 4.3 Realizing the Recursive Overview

To aid understanding, the previous section deliberately abstracted away from the concrete RLWE instanti- ation and presented the recursion in a clean algebraic form. To instantiate this interface, each level must preserve the same input/output shape while handling three concrete issues: message and ciphertext objects live in different domains and ciphertext evaluations introduce noise. Also, the concrete evaluation should be optimized to make the scheme practical. Section 6 gives the lower-level roadmap, and Sections 6.1, 6.2 and 7 develop these components. Matching domains.The first issue appears at the boundary between two recursive levels. Expansion at one level needsRto hold the short digest-side relation in the first form below, whileShrinkExpandproduces the output-side relation in the second form:

*w* *′* *w* skx=skr*·*dx+sk*k∈C,*mx= ∆*·*x+k*∈M.*

The recursive sketch reuses the first relation (skx) as a smaller instance of the second. This reuse is type- correct only if digests and keys can be represented as messages for the next shrink/expand call. In the concrete scheme, however, digests andLHEkeys are in*C*, while the messages are in*M*. We handle this mismatch by using CRT decomposition to represent each*C*element as residues that fit the message domain, running shrink/expand on those residues, and recombining the results after expansion. Because CRT preserves addition and multiplication, the decomposed relations carry the samedx*·*skrproduct as the original relation. Section 6.1 gives the details. Controlling noise.Once the interface is type-correct, the next obstruction is correctness of the ciphertext evaluations inside each recursive level. In our recursive computation, every level evaluatesLacEandLHE ciphertexts on digests. A direct multiplication by a digest*d∈Rq*would use large coefficients and can push LHEnoise past the decryption bound, damaging the messages inside. We therefore replace this multiplication by products againstg *−*1

(*d*), whose digits are bounded by a small gadget base*g*, to avoid blowing up the noise.
Also, after each level, the parties scale-and-round their decrypted shares, as in many LWE-based schemes, to produce noise-free shares of the needed keys, so evaluation noise is cleared and does not grow with recursion depth. Finally, we also keep the gadget-decomposition compatible with the CRT-decomposition used in shrink/expand across recursion levels. Section 6.2 explains how the gadget digits and CRT components are handled together. Optimizing the concrete cost.Now, the remaining work is to reduce the dominant concrete costs without changing what each level exports. We propose two optimizations: First, one-digit gadget truncation reduces the overhead from gadget decomposition: for our implementation choice*τ*= 2, it reduces one-time setup communication by about 4*×*and query computation by about 2*×*. Second, golden-seed grinding uses rejection sampling to reduce the ring-element size from*O*(*λ³*) to*O*(*λ*log² *|*x*|*). Section 7 gives the details.

### 4.4 Supporting Non-interactiveCI-VOLE

Now we discuss how to modify the above interactive protocol to obtain a non-interactive public-key version. In this setting,Sprepares the reusable setup material (ctr*,*ctlac) with its chosen global key ∆. Then, it publishes it as a public key, while keeping the secret state (skr*,*∆). OnceRobtains the public key, it can generate the correlation*m*xaccording to its chosen inputx. Then,Rcan send a single message toSto letS recover the matching shareksuch that*m*x*−*k= ∆*·*x. Thus one sender public key for a fixed ∆ can serve many receiver executions; the protocol domain-separates these executions by their identifiers. Our observation enabling the non-interactive version is that the only online communication is the ping-

### 4.5 Ideal Functionality and Modeling

Our basic functionality is the basic CI-VOLE interface in Figure 1. The sender fixes a global correlation value ∆*∈M*and a width*w*. On a receiver inputx*∈M* *w*, the functionality samples a fresh sender mask k*←M* *w*, giveskto the sender, and gives the receiver

m=k+x*·*∆*∈M* *w* *.*

Since this overview focuses on the high-level ideas, this functionality idealizes only the correlation genera- tion needed by applications such as the ZKP. The generalized functionality in Section 8 further idealizes the protocol’s ability to generate correlation across multiple sessions, run non-interactively, and let the sender pre-compute its shares.

### 4.6 Application: IT-MAC-based ZKP

Prior IT-MAC-based ZKP protocols [WYKW21, BMRS21, DIO21a, YSWW21, DILO22, LHLA25] first obtain authenticated shares (labels) on a random vector under the verifier’s hidden key ∆. The proverP then sends a*linear-size*derandomization vector to move those labels to the actual extended witness,*i.e.*, the execution trace for the verification circuit. In our instantiation,VisCI-VOLE’s sender, andPis the receiver. The point ofLogVoleis that the labels are generated directly onP’s chosen witness, skipping the linear-size derandomization communication. We have two routes to a maliciously secure ZKP. The first route supports reusable public-key setup material forLogVole. Here, the parties use the maliciousLogVolevariant: the succinct public key is certified once using a lattice-based ZKP to achieve security againstV, and manyPexecutions can reuse that key under different session IDs. Security against maliciousPrelies on the extractability ofLogVole’s digest. The second route uses a (simpler) fresh setup.Vgenerates a fresh semi-honestLogVolesetup for a single proof session. To obtain malicious security againstV, afterPcommits to the proof generated from the LogVoleoutput,Vopens the setup randomness,Pchecks that the setup was honestly generated and then opens its committed proofs. For malicious security againstP, the protocol applies a random-linear check to P’s output to ensure that the fixed transcript binds to one effective trace. It then uses a standard IT-MAC check to verify that this witness satisfies the verification circuit. Sections F and 11 presents the concrete ZKP protocol and proof sketch.

## 5 The Shrink/Expand Core

This section prepares the reader for the low-level overview in Section 6 and Section 7. It opens the box of the coreShrink*/*Expandmechanism, a critical component of our protocol, and focuses on its algebraic structure. We do not introduce any new ideas from our work here. Basic Terminology.In general, a value*m*=*s·x*+*k*is an authenticated label of*x*, or equivalently an IT-MAC of*x*; we use the two terms interchangeably. Here, the sender owns the authentication key*s*and the additive mask*k*, while the receiver owns the label*m*and the authenticated value*x*.

The reusable materials (ct*,*ct) are prepared independently ofx. Indeed, we crucially rely on the fact that the same materials can be used to translate many different (*d* (1)*,*x)*,...,*(*d* (*µ*)*,*x) pairs. In that sense, these materials act as a vector of reusable garbled gates for this degree-1 label-translation map. These gates can be evaluated for*all*valid (*d,*x) pairs. In contrast, evaluation effectively outputs*⊥*for any input that is not a valid digest-preimage pair. This remarkable construction originates inTinyLabel[DLL25]. We repeat it here because it is integral to our construction and to the various optimizations that interact with it. The rest of this section explains how the structure of the digest itself enables*bounded openings*that allow the translation to be performed only for valid digest-preimage pairs.

### 5.4 Bounded Openings Enable Translation

As seen above, the point of the digest is not merely to compress a block to a root. For our digest, the translation only works for the input that actually produced the root. When the receiver computes*d*x, it also computes, for every edge on the path to a leaf*x*ind *i*

|||, a bounded openingw|satisfying||
|---|---|---|---|---|
||i−1|ind i|i b i i i|i|
||||i i||
|(i+1) i x|ind|||i|
||i i′|i′|i−1||

*y* =*−Bw, yi*=*−G*b*iw.* P The equations alone are not enough. In the telescope, the error is E [ind]*w*, so the row noise remains controlled only when the lifted opening is bounded. A large algebraic preimage may have the right formal shape, but it does not yield a usable noisy translation: the noiseE [ind]*w* is large when*wi*is large, and this destroys the payload*r y*. Thus the receiver cannot take a root digest and freely decide which leaves it opens to. To translate the root’s authenticated digest label down to*x*, it needs the bounded openings*w* tied to the same digest computation that produced*d*. If a receiver could translate the same root toward a different set of leaves, it would have found a second bounded opening*w*. Under the SIS assumption, no PPT adversary can produce two distinct bounded openings*w,w* of one node digest*y*, except with negligible probability; see Theorems 5 and 6.

### 5.5 How the Pieces Compose

The localShrink*/*Expandidentity has two properties that make our high-level recursive protocol succinct. First, theLacEandLHEmaterials are reusable. TheLacEciphertextsC*i*inctlac, and in particular their selected rowsC*i*[ind], are fixed in the one-time setup and are independent of the receiver’s later blockx,

|i|||
|---|---|---|
|i||r|

the digest values*y*, and the bounded openings*wi*. Similarly, theLHEbridge ciphertextct is fixed in the one-time setup and can be reused with the digest value of any block at that level. The query input only selects bounded openings on a valid digest tree; the same materials can therefore be reused across all of the blocks in a given level of the protocol-recursion tree. Second, the identity is self-similar. A recursive call produces authenticated shares for the decomposed digest inputs dˆx. After recombination,Expandturns each authenticated digest label into the block IT-MAC formm=s*⊙*x+k, which has the same form expected at the next level. Thus the protocol repeatedly shrinks blocks to digest inputs, obtains authenticated shares recursively, and expands those shares back to the original blocks. The striking point is that these are not separate mechanisms. The same translation identity from TinyLabelmoves the multiplicative factor down a digest path and makes the row evaluation useful only when the receiver supplies bounded openings for the digest tree. Reuse and self-similarity then turn this identity into the logarithmic chosen-input VOLE protocol.

### 5.6 The True Shrink/Expand Interface

The simplified interface above is the one-ring picture of the mechanism. The actual recursive interface uses the same cancellation pattern, but the object passed between recursive levels is not the bare digest*d*x. For a blockx*∈R* *µ* *q*, the shrink step first computes

*d*x:=LacE*.*Digest(x)*∈Rq.*

Thus the real interface replaces the single authenticated digest labele*m*=e*sd*x+ e*k*by decomposed label pieces that match the ciphertext modulus, gadget basis, and CRT coordinates used by the next recursive call. The row translation still performs the same cancellation proved above; the lower-level realization adds the representation changes, noise handling, and rounding needed to make the cancellation correct in the actual parameter domains. Section 6 gives this realization: CRT decomposition appears in Section 6.1, gadget decomposition and noise handling in Section 6.2, and digest randomization in Section 6.4. The formal protocol boxes are given in Figures 9 and 10; Section 7 then describes the concrete optimizations.

### 6.1 CRT-Decomposition for Type Mismatch

While the high-level recursive strategy is intuitive, realizing it requires overcoming subtle technical challenges. A critical issue arises from the mismatch between the plaintext and ciphertext spaces. Conversion betweenZ*p*and*Rp*.We start by clarifying the type of the messages and resulting shares inCI-VOLE. For our intended applications such as ZK and GC, message space*M*=Z*p*. However, the underlyingLHEandLacEschemes operate over the ring*M*=*Rp*as inTinyLabel.TinyLabelsolves it by encoding a vector of messagesZ *n* *p*into the encryption scheme plaintext*Rp*via the Chinese Remainder Theorem (CRT). After obtaining the authenticated shares in*Rq*, it applies inverse CRT to reconstruct the shares inZ*p*. The general case ofCI-VOLEoverZ*p*reduces toCI-VOLEover*Rp*via the wrapper in Figure 3. Henceforth, we treat all values as ring elements in*Rq*or*Rp*. Modulus Mismatch.There is an important modulus mismatch: the ciphertexts of the underlyingLHE andLacEschemes operate over*C*=*Rq*while the messages operate over*M*=*Rp*with*q≫p*. In other words, the digest and keys operate in a much larger ciphertext modulus*q*than the message modulus*p*. When the parties try to input the digests and keys in*Rq*into the next level of shrink/expand, it simply fails because the mechanism expects inputs in*Rp*. The gap between the modulus*p*and*q*is essential for underlying RLWE-basedLHEandLacEbecause these schemes inject small noise into the ciphertexts to ensure security. After evaluation on the ciphertext, the noise grows. Thus, these schemes encode the message in the higher bits in the ciphertext to leave enough room for noise growth in the lower bits, ensuring the correctness of decryption. Thus, we must have*q≫p* to prevent the noise from growing into the higher bits and causing decryption failure. A na¨ıve solution is to enlarge the next level’s message modulus to be*p* *′* =*q*and increase the ciphertext modulus to*q* *′* *≫p* *′* =*q*accordingly. However, with recursion, these blowups compound multiplicatively, resulting in ciphertext sizes exponential in the recursion depth and linear in*|*x*|*. Another issue with using different moduli is that each level of shrink/expand gadgets must useLacEand LHEwith different parameters, forcingSto send different (ctlac*,*ctr) and introducing another logarithmic factor in the communication. Hence, for the efficiency of our scheme, we want to keep the message modulus*p*and ciphertext modulus *q*constant across all levels of recursion. Our CRT-based Decomposition Solution.The high-level idea is to decompose the digest and keys in*Rq*into smaller values in*Rp*using CRT decomposition⁵. Since CRT preserves the algebraic structure (*i.e.*, addition and multiplication) after conversion, the resulting values still maintain the desired algebraic relations (*i.e.*,skx=dx*·*sk₁ +sk*k*).

Here, CRT decomposes coefficients into smaller magnitudes, whereas inTinyLabel, CRT maps integers to ring elements.

Q Concretely, the parties sample*ρ*NTT-friendly primes*q₁,...,qρ*and set the ciphertext modulus as*q*= *i∈*[*ρ*]*qi*. Thanks to the underlyingLacEandLHE, the parties can use different plaintext moduli*p*= *q₁,...,qρ*in different ciphertexts.

A na¨ıve solution is to instantiate*ρ*independentShrinkExpandinstances, where the*i*-th instance handles queries in*Rqi*. Yet, it will also inflate the number of digests by a factor of*ρ*, which in turn increases the input number of the next level’sShrinkExpandby a factor of*ρ*and causes a*ρ²*-blowup in the ciphertext size. 6

We propose an optimized approach using a singleShrinkExpand*R*instance, requiring only*O*(*αρ*) ci- phertexts for a shrinking factor*α*=*w/w* *′*. Here,d andsk₁ are each duplicated*α*times, yielding output in*R*. As is standard in BFV-like schemes, sk₁ is multiplied by*δ* =*q /q* to lift the message for noise tolerance. Upon receiving the output from ShrinkExpand, the parties scale down the*i*-th chunk (of*ρ*elements) by*δ* and round, effectively obtaining the result modulo*q*. Concrete Efficiency.We emphasize that this decomposition has only small impact on practical perfor- mance. For efficiency, BFV-like schemes typically use CRT to represent large ciphertext words as smaller (*≤*64-bit) machine words; thus, constructing*q*as a product of small primes is standard practice. In our evaluation, we use a small*ρ*= 4. Cost Accounting.The CRT layer changes the width of each recursiveShrinkExpandblock to*µ*=*αρτ*. For an input length*w*=*τρα* *ℓ*, the offline recursion has*ℓ*= log*α*(*w/*(*τρ*)) =*O*(log*αw*) levels. Summing the non-root setup over these levels gives

#### O(logαw·αρτ·log(αρ)·|Rq|+ logαw·λ)

bits of communication. For computation, we countMult*R*because full ring multiplications dominate integer operations, ring additions, and CRT bookkeeping. The matching offline computation is

To maintain the shrinking ratio of*α*, eachShrinkExpandinstance should process*ρ·α*inputs. Together with*ρ*instances, the total ciphertext count increases to*O*(*ρ ·α*).

### 6.2 Gadget Decomposition for Noise Handling

Thus far, we have omitted the details of noise management inLHEschemes. While addressing noise introduces some complexity, the overall structure of our construction remains the same. We assume familiarity with standard lattice-basedLHEschemes such as BGV or BFV. The core issue arises inShrinkExpand. To enable recursion and reduce problem size,Rcomputes LHE*.*Enc(skx*·d*x*,*r*·d*x) by evaluatingct₁ *·d*x. However, since*d*xis a pseudo-random element in*Rq*with large coefficients, this multiplication amplifies the noise inct₁ beyond the decryption bound, destroying the plaintext messages inr*·d*xand rendering the resulting ciphertext invalid.

Gadget Decomposition.To address this,TinyLabelemploys the gadget decomposition technique. Con- sequently, our “compressed” decryption key is no longer a share of the digest multiplied by a global key, but rather a share of the inner product of the decomposed digests. We detail this below. FollowingTinyLabel, theLHEpublic parameters include a small integer base*g*, chosen such that the noise budget ofct₁ tolerates multiplication by P

## 7 Optimizations for Practical Efficiency

### 7.1 One-Digit Gadget Truncation

Recall that in Section 6.1, we explained that our construction requires gadget decomposition to contain the noise growth inLacEandLHEevaluations. The gadget decomposition is the main source of the*τ*-factor overhead in the heaviest objects in the protocol, particularly in theLacEciphertextctlac. Here is a simple calculation: ourShrinkExpandconstruction with input size*µ*=*α·τ·ρ*has*µ*log₂ *µ*nodes in the laconic ciphertexts, and each node has*τ*ciphertexts for the gadget decomposition. Hence, there are *O*(*τ²* log*τ*) ciphertexts for the setup. In terms of computation, eachShrinkExpandrequires*µ·O*(*τ*log*τ*) ring operations. Combining with the fact that processing aCI-VOLEquery requires 2*·w/µ*invocations of ShrinkExpand, the computation overhead of a query is*w·O*(*τ*log*τ*) ring operations. With our truncation optimization, we can reduce the*τ*factor in overhead to*τ−*1,*i.e.*, replacing*τ*by *τ−*1 in the above cost calculations. Concretely, as we use*τ*= 2 in our implementation, this optimization reduces the setup communication by*≈*4*×*and computation by*≈*2*×*.

At the high level, our trick is to omit the least-significant gadget digit in theLHEandLacEevaluations. This introduces additional error in the resulting ciphertexts, but it can still be absorbed into the existing noise margin.

Security.Most changes in this optimization can be understood as skipping the least-significant gadget digit in their evaluations, and thus do not affect the security. The security-relevant change is thatLacEis now encrypted using masks*{*r

(*i*) *}* *i∈*[*ℓ*]from the error distribution *D*instead of the uniform distribution in
*Rq*. A concern is that an adversary might be able to find a collision of the digests, allowing a malicious Rto use two different inputsxandx *′* but with similar digests*d*xand*d*x*′* such that the high-digit part of the digest is the same and (*d*x*−d*x*′*)ris small enough to preserve the message. Then, thisRcan locally evaluateShrinkExpandfor these two inputs and obtain the correlated randomness for both inputs using the same decryption keyskx, and then solve for ∆. Fortunately, even with the short*{*r

(*i*) *}*, our protocol still enjoys the same security guarantee as the
full-gadget version. It is computationally hard for the adversary to find such collisions under a variant of SIS assumption, which can be reduced to the standard SIS assumption with a slight loss in the security parameter [DSH21]. In other words, for a fixed decryption keyskx,Ris still computationally bound to an inputxas in the full-gadget version.

### 7.2 Golden-Seed Grinding

The ideas in the overview (Section 4 and Section 6) achieve an asymptotic communication complexity *O*(*λ³*). Here, we go one step further and introduce an optimization that can reduce the communication cost to*O*(*λ*log² *|*x*|*) and enable us to use smaller RLWE parameters for better concrete performance.

Background about avoidingct*k*transmission.We first explain the RO optimization inTinyLabelused to avoid transmitting the large ciphertextct*k*. To sample a randomct*k*without transmitting it, both parties agree on a nonce*v*and setct*k*[*j*] =*H*(*v||j*) for each*j∈*[*w* *′*] where*w* *′* is the number of ring elements inct*k*. The ciphertext has the formct*k*=a*·*sk*k*+*δ·*msg+*e*as in BFV-like schemes, whereais the public random element,sk*k*is the secret key,*δ*=*q/p*is the noise scaling factor,msgis the message,*e*is the noise term, and*p*and*q*are the message and ciphertext moduli, respectively. However, an important nuance is thatct*k*is uniformly chosen, so*e*is also uniformly distributed in*Rδ*. Then,ct*k*is added toct*r·d*xandctlacto produce the final ciphertext

ctres=ct*k*+ct*r·d*x+ctlac

The issue is that if the noise inctres,*i.e.*,

noise(ctres) = noise(ct*k*) + noise(ct*r*)*·g* *−*1 (*d*x) + noise(ctlac)

is too large, decryption will fail, meaningRcannot obtain the correct share ofx*·*∆. To resolve this issue, TinyLabelproposes a theoretical solution, where the parties estimate the noise bound*B*ofct*r·g* *−*1 (*d*x) and ctlacand ensure that there is an exponential gap 2 *λ* between*δ*and*B*,*i.e.*,*δ > B·*2 *λ*, to ensure decryption correctness. Only if the noise inct*k*is in the “bad intervals” 8 that are very close to*δ/*2, the noise from ct*r·d*x+ctlac*< B*could nudgectres’s noise over*δ/*2 and cause decryption failure, which happens with*<*2 *−λ*

probability. While it achieves good asymptotic communication by eliminating the transmission ofct*k*, it requires log*q*= Θ(*λ*). It further inflates the ring degree*n*=*O*(*λ*log*q*) =*O*(*λ²*) for security [ACC + 19] and thus the ring element size to*O*(*n*log*q*) =*O*(*λ³*), leading to larger communication and computation costs.

TinyLabel’s Rejection Sampling and Issues.The goal now is to arrive at a moderate set of RLWE parameters for good concrete efficiency. Instead of having a construction entirelyct*k*-free,TinyLabeluses rejection sampling to strike a middle ground where for each*coefficient*inct*k*,Sonly sends a few bits. Specifically, on top of a seed*ς*(serving as a nonce), the parties use a counter value*di,j*to derive each coefficient ofct*k*asct*k*[*i,j*] =*H*(*ς||i||j||di,j*). Notably,Smust ensurect*k*[*i,j*]’s noise is in the “good interval” [*−δ/*2 +*B,δ/*2*−B*]. Hence,Swould use itssk*k*to decrypt the sampledct*k*[*i,j*] to learn the noise value. If the noise is too large, it increments*di,j*and resamplesct*k*[*i,j*] until its noise falls in the good interval. After the sampling,Sadditionally sends (*di,j*)*i,j*toR. Because correctness is guaranteed by resampling, the gap between*δ*and*B*need not be 2 *λ* but rather a small constant 2. Each coefficient on average requires 2 sampling attempts, meaning sending (*di,j*)*i,j*requires*≈*2*N*bits where*N*is the total number of coefficients inct*k*. While it achieves good concrete efficiency, sending*≈*2*N*=*O*(*|*x*|*) bits immediately defeats our purpose of achieving succinct communication.

Our Golden-Seed Trick.Here, we introduce our “golden-seed” grinding trick to achieve the best of both worlds: we eliminate the additive*λ*overhead in log*q*while keeping the communication cost succinct. In hindsight, this trick is surprisingly straightforward and can be seen as a generalization of the rejection sampling trick. Our idea is that instead of setting the gap between*δ*and*B*to be either 2 *λ* or 2, we set it to be 2*cN* for a constant*c≥*1. Now, the parties derivect*k*using a global seed*ς*like before but without the per- coefficient rejection counters,*i.e.*,ct*k*[*i,j*] =*H*(*ς||i||j*)*∈*Z*q*where*q*= 2*cN·Bp*. Now, because the gap is 2*cN*, the probability that a single coefficient falls into the bad intervals is*P*= 2*B/*(2*cNB*) = 1*/*(*cN*). Given thatct*k*has*N*coefficients in total, a seed yields all “good” coefficients with probability approximately (1*−P*) *N* *≈*exp(*−PN*) = exp(*−*1*/c*). With a natural choice*c*= 1, the success probability is exp(*−*1)*≈*37%. In the unfortunate case that the seed is not “golden”,*i.e.*, at least one coefficient falls into the bad intervals, Ssimply discards the seed and tries the next one. On average,Sonly needs to try (1*−P*) *−N* *≈*exp(1*/c*) seeds to find a golden one. For*c*= 1,Sneeds to try*≈*2*.*718 seeds. The concrete sampling routine used byLogVole*R*is specified in Figure 5. It computes the public noise budget*B*, hashes candidate seeds from (id*,d*rt*,k*rt), and rejects a candidate whenever one of the induced <u>correction ciphertexts exceeds the bound.</u>

more precisely, the bad intervals are [*−δ/*2 +*B,−δ/*2)*∪*(*δ/ −B,δ/*2) over its entire noise interval [*−δ/,δ/*2)

Cost Reduction.The additional communication cost is*λ*for sending the seed. But now the parties can choose a smaller*q*no longer scaling with 2 *λ* but rather*q*=*O*(log*|*x*|*) as*N≈*2*|*x*|*in our binary-tree-like recursion. Consequently, the ring degree*n*=*O*(*λq*) =*O*(*λ*log² *|*x*|*) is also smaller, resulting in a lower communication cost of*n·q*=*O*(*λ*log² *|*x*|*). The smaller ring elements also reduce the concrete computation cost.

Integrating with Our Recursion Scheme.The above idea focuses on optimizingTinyLabel. We further apply it to our recursion scheme, which is essentially a recursion tree where every node is a (TinyLabel-like) ShrinkExpandinstance.Scan sample a golden seed in the offline phase because the sampling trick only requiresS’s knowledge ofsk*k*and the public parameters. To be more specific, recall thatScan sample a sk*k*at the root level and then expand it recursively by decryptingct*k*with thesk*k*of the parent level in offline phase. Hence,Scan sample a single seed used for everyShrinkExpandinstance’s hashing in aLogVole invocation. If any hashing in a node results in a bad coefficient,Sresamples the seed. The only caveat is that the gap between*δ*and*B*becomes 2*cN*, where*N*is the total number of coefficients in the entire recursion tree.

Security.In the random-oracle model, the seed defines the same correction ciphertexts that would oth- erwise be sampled coefficient-wise, except that we condition on the same good-interval predicate used by TinyLabel’s rejection sampling. This conditioning only exposes that the published ciphertexts are valid for correctness; it does not add leakage beyond the rejection information already present inTinyLabel. Since a golden seed is found with constant probability, the simulator can sample or program the random oracle with only constant expected overhead. The rest of the transcript is unchanged, so security continues to rely on the same RLWE,LacE, andLHEassumptions as the underlyingTinyLabel-likeShrinkExpandprotocol.

Figure 6: Generalized CI-VOLE functionality. The two modes capture the timing patterns used by our

applications: non-interactive calls let the receiver reroll before release, while interactive calls fixxandk separately before releasingm.

## 8 Generalized CI-VOLE Functionality

Figure 1 gives the basic semi-honest functionality used to state the core idea: after the sender fixes ∆, the

receiver choosesx, and the functionality sampleskand returns the correlated outputs. The full protocol needs a slightly more flexible API because different applications require different timing. In the non-interactive setting, the receiver may try candidate calls before deciding which one to release to the sender. In the interactive setting, the sender-side maskkmay have to be available before the receiver’sx-dependent output is released. The generalized functionality in Figure 6 exposes exactly these two timing patterns while preserving the same underlying VOLE relationm=k+x*·*∆.

## 9 The Construction

This section summarizes our protocols. Due to space constraints, we present only the core CI-VOLE interface and defer the lower-level mechanisms and proofs to the appendix.

### 9.1 Core CI-VOLE overRq

The reusable one-time setup and query phases are given in Figures 9 and 10. At a high level, the one-time setup phase prepares the sender and receiver state, while the query phase generates correlations for chosen inputs.

Theorem 2(Semi-honest summary).*Assume that the Ring-*LWE*assumption holds for the parameter regime* *used by the underlying*LacE*and*LHE*schemes, and consider the programmable random-oracle model. Then* *the protocol in Figures 9 and 10 correctly realizesFCI-VOLEoverRqand is secure against semi-honest adver-* *saries.*

*Proof sketch.*This follows from Theorems 8 and 9 in Appendix Section B.

Deferred Details.Due to space constraints, we defer the detailed discussion of the machinery that makes this recursion work: theZ*p*-to-*Rq*wrapper, the CRT and gadget decompositions, the noise-management argument, and the digest properties used in the security proofs. These are explained in Sections A, B, 6 and 7. Semi-Honest Security.The values ∆ held bySandxheld byRare their private inputs. Theorem 9 proves that the transcript hides both values except through the prescribed outputs. Wrapper overZ*p*.Our intended applications (*e.g.*, ZKPs) work overZ*p*, so we batch field elements into ring slots, invoke the ring protocol, and unbatch the resulting shares. The wrapper is shown in Figure 3; we defer it and its analysis to Sections B and 6.1.

## 10 Malicious Security

The semi-honest construction in Figures 9 and 10 does not by itself specify the extra checks needed against active parties. This section records the protocol-level changes used by the malicious variant. The protocol ideas and relations are given here; Section E then describes the corresponding simulators, extraction routes, and hybrids.

### 10.1 Protocol-Level Changes

The malicious protocol is obtained by making the following local changes to the semi-honest protocol: we disable the golden-seed optimization, certify the sender’s reusable setup packages, use a no-seed interactive online response, and optionally append a receiver-side random-linear check.

10.1.1 No-Golden Seed Mode Our malicious-security proof is for the variant that does not use the golden-seed optimization of Section 7.2. The optimized semi-honest protocol callsFindGoldenSeed(id*,k*rt
*′* *,d* *′* rt) so that the seed-derived correction ci- phertexts avoid the bad noise intervals. Against a malicious sender, that would create an extra validity obligation: the receiver would need to know that the advertised seed was actually good for the hidden *k*rt *′*. We do not currently prove that obligation. Accordingly, the malicious theorem is stated for the no- golden variant below. In this variant, the sender does not runFindGoldenSeed, and the receiver performs no golden-seed verification. This is a parameter tradeoff, not an additional protocol check. The malicious theorem is stated for param- eters where arbitrary seed-derived or hash-derived correction ciphertexts are correct except with negligible probability, using the no-golden modulus gap described in Section 7.2. For the concrete parameter regime used in our evaluation, we estimate that moving to this no-golden regime would increase both computation

Instantiating the sender proofs.The sender-side setup statement is a structured statement about short witnesses to exact modular linear relations. For setup, after flattening ring elements into coefficient vectors overZ*q*, the algebraic part has the form

*M*setup*w*setup=*u*setup(mod*q*)*.*

The rows of*M* encode the bridge-opening equations, the endpoint equations for the carried-mask chains,

|setup|||
|---|---|---|
|ring br|row||

and the row-programming equations for the public LacE ciphertext rows. The proof also enforces the concrete bounds Γ*,*Γ*,*Γ on the corresponding witness coordinates. We instantiate these proofs using the Fiat–Shamir NIZK argument-of-knowledge interface from Sec- tion 3.8, using lattice proofs for bounded solutions to exact modular linear equations in the style of Bootle– Lyubashevsky–Seiler and the later LNP framework [BLS19, LNP22]. For any public modulus*q*, public matrix*M*, public target*u*, and public coordinate-wise bound vector Γ, the proof system gives a proof*π*for the predicate*R*lin(*M,u,*Γ;*w*), which checks*Mw*=*u*(mod*q*) and*∥wt∥∞≤*Γ*t*for every bounded coordinate

*t*. The malicious theorem pays a union bound over all setup-package proofs. We apply this interface once to each arity-2 ordinary setup statement and once to the arity-4 widened root setup statement, using the bounds inpp
(*h*) orpp rt. This baseline certifies the modular decomposition directly; batching across leaves,
recursion levels, or repeated bridge instances can be added later without changing the malicious protocol interface.

10.1.5 Receiver-Input Extraction Choices The sender-side setup proofs are always part of the malicious protocol. For receiver-input extraction, we allow two operational variants. The first variant makes no additional receiver-side protocol change: the protocol runs with only the certified sender setup above, and the proof relies on the digest-knowledge route. Informally, if a receiver later uses a public digest successfully, then the only useful way to do so is through bounded digest openings that determine the effective leaves; the simulator extracts those leaves and submits them to the ideal functionality. This is the no-message path, and its knowledge-extraction assumption is isolated in Section E.4. The second variant is the explicit random-linear-check wrapper below. It is more conventional and easier to state as a standalone protocol compiler, but it sacrifices a few final output positions and adds a small final consistency check. Both variants serve the same role in the malicious proof: after the receiver transcript is fixed, the simulator obtains one effective input*x*
eff for the final CI-VOLE output.

10.1.6 Optional Receiver-Side Random-Linear Check The base malicious proof can use the digest-knowledge extraction route, which adds no receiver-side protocol message. As a more conventional alternative, we can append a random-linear-check adaptor to the final VOLE output. This check is used only to bind and extract the receiver’s effective input; it does not change the shrink/expand core. In this variant, the check challenge is carried alongside the online response. In interactive mode, the sender samples an independent*ρ*chkand sends
(skrt *′* *,ρ*chk)*.*

The coefficients for the final check are derived from*ρ*chk. Thus the interactive check uses an honestly sampled statistical challenge, while the non-interactive version relies on the usual computational Fiat– Shamir/random-oracle treatment. We describe the adaptor for an application output of length*w*. The protocol is run for a total length

The receiver holds*x,m*, the sender holds ∆*,k*, and the random linear combinations below are taken over the first*w*application coordinates. If an application prefers the top-level field formulation, the same wrapper can instead be applied after the final field projection. Let*T*be the challenge-free prefix for the check. In the interactive mode, this is all protocol messages up to and including the receiver obtainingskrt *′*, but not the check challenge. In the non-interactive mode, this is the transcript up to the online random-oracle query*d* *′* rt, together with fixed first two answer components (skrt *′* *,ς*); each probe answers the same query with these fixed components and a fresh challenge field. The challenge seed*ρ*chkis sampled by the sender in interactive mode, and is the third component of*H*on(id*,d* *′* rt) in non-interactive mode. The hash hides the sender’s checked value, while the scalar check-VOLE masks the receiver’s random linear view from the sender. No separate check-VOLE source is needed; the check correlations are ordinary output coordinates that are reserved for this purpose. For soundness parameter*λ*, choose*τ*chk=*⌈λ/β*chk*⌉*, where*β*chk is the per-check soundness in bits of the chosen linear hash family over*R* *w*

*q*. For example, if one works after a
field projection to a field of size 2 *b*, then fully random coefficients give*β*chk=*b*; for a polynomial/power hash over an output of length*w*, the usual almost-universality loss reduces this by about*⌈*log₂ *w⌉*. Thus a check domain with 128 bits of per-check soundness needs one repetition for 128-bit soundness, whereas smaller checked domains require enough independent repetitions that the accumulated check entropy reaches*λ*. For extraction, the simulator rewinds after this challenge-free prefix is fixed, keeps the sameskrt *′*, and uses fresh independent check challenges. In the non-interactive mode this means reprogramming only the challenge field of the online random-oracle answer. In an accepting rewind, the receiver message gives the affine equation X
