chk )*.*

Solving this system recovers the application part of the effective input. The formal extraction argument is carried out in the corrupted-receiver hybrid proof of Theorem 11. The extraction proof also needs a binding condition. The check alone would not rule out a receiver that somehow prepares two complete VOLE explanations for the same public transcript, one for*x*and one for *x* *′* *̸*=*x*, and answers consistently with either one. The random-linear-check proof does not use digest binding to exclude this pathology. It uses a protocol-specific binding fact: after the sender-side ciphertexts have been simulated, the receiver’s transcript is independent of ∆, while two distinct final-output VOLE explanations under the same sender mask give a nontrivial equation involving ∆. Thus a receiver that can keep both

explanations alive would effectively predict a fresh authenticated relation involving ∆, which occurs only with negligible probability in the full proof. The proof section treats the prefix*T*, the hash family, the number of rewinds, and the ∆-independence condition as explicit parameters of the extraction lemma.

## 11 IT-MAC-based ZKP

The ZK application usesLogVoleto remove the linear derandomization step from VOLE-based IT-MAC proofs. Prior systems first obtain random authenticated shares and then send a correction vector for the prover’s actual execution trace. Here, the prover instead usesLogVoleto obtain authenticated shares directly on its chosen trace. There are two ways to justify this step, depending on whether the verifier’s setup is long lived.

Reusable public-key route.If the verifier wants to publish one reusable public key for many future provers, the ZK protocol should use the maliciousCI-VOLEvariant of Section 10. That route keeps the public- key interface: the verifier publishes certified sender-side setup for a fixed ∆, and many prover executions can use it with domain-separated identifiers. The price is exactly the malicious-VOLE machinery: sender-side consistency proofs and receiver-input extraction, instantiated either by the digest-knowledge route or by the random-linear-check wrapper of Section 10.1.6.

Opened fresh-setup route.The implementation in this paper uses a simpler designated-verifier route. For each proof session, the verifier generates a fresh semi-honestLogVolesetup, using the optimized protocol including the golden-seed optimization. No sender-side NIZK is attached to this setup. Instead, after the prover has committed to the proof values that must be fixed before seeing ∆, the verifier opens the setup randomness: ∆, encryption randomness, noise, seeds, and the other coins needed to recompute the prover- visible setup. The prover checks that the setup was honestly generated and only then opens its committed proof values. This opening step is useful because it replaces a proof of setup well-formedness by direct verification. It is not suitable for a long-lived public key, since the verifier reveals ∆ at the end of the session. But it is ideal for the implemented designated-verifier protocol: it lets the ZK proof use the heavily optimized semi-honest LogVolelayer, while still preventing a malformed verifier setup from entering the soundness argument.

Binding the prover to one trace.The only remaining issue is prover soundness. A malicious prover might try to use oneLogVoletranscript before the verifier opens ∆, and then reinterpret that transcript as authenticated labels for a different execution trace afterwards. We rule this out with the same random- linear-check idea used in Section 10.1.6. TheLogVoleinvocation includes a few sacrificed output coordinates. After the prover’sLogVoletranscript is fixed, the verifier samples fresh random linear challenges. The prover answers linear checks against the authenticated output, using the sacrificed coordinates as one-time pads for the checked linear views. Rewinding on fresh challenges extracts one effective trace except with the usual 1*/p*soundness loss per independent field check. Once this effective trace is fixed, the rest of the proof is the standard IT-MAC checking layer. The prover forms the extended witnessx= (*x₁,...,xN*), obtains labels

*mj*=*kj*+*xj*∆*j∈*[*N*]*,*

and proves that these labeled values satisfy the circuit. Addition and output checks are affine. For a multiplication gate*xaxb*=*xc*, define *ϵ*:=*xaxb−xc.*

With *V₂* :=*m*

|U:=k|k +k|∆,|m,|V₁ :=m −x|m −x|m,|

||a b|c|a b|c|a b|b a|
|||||2|||

the verifier equation is *U*=*V₂* +*V₁*∆ +*ϵ*∆*.*

## 12 Performance and Evaluation

### 12.1 Cost Analysis

In this section, we analyze the cost of our optimized, interactive constructionLogVole. This accounting is meant to be read after the lower-level appendix material in Section 6 (Lower-Level Realization) and Section 7 (optimizations). In particular, Section 6.1 details the cost ofShrinkExpand, a critical component ofLogVole. Basic Parameters.We count the interactive protocol with the golden-seed optimization (Section 7.2), shown in Figures 3, 7, 9 and 10. Let*N*:=*|*x*|*be the number of requestedZ*p*-labels, and let*m*msg:= log*p* be the bit length of aZ*p*-label. Let*w*:=*⌈N/n⌉*be the number of ring elements after CRT batching.*|Rq|*= *n*log*q*is a ring element’s size. Golden-seed grinding gives log*q*=*O*(*m*msg+ log*N*); with*n*=*O*(*λ*log*q*), we have*|Rq|*=*O*(*λ*(*m*msg+ log*N*) 2 ) bits. We use*τ*for the gadget width. Set*µ*:=*αρτ*as the batch width of one non-rootShrinkExpandblock: each block shrinks by*α*, carries*ρ*CRT residues, and uses*τ*gadget digits. Note that*w*=*τρα* *ℓ* for*ℓ*= log*α*(*w/*(*τρ*)). Also, the constants*α,ρ,τ*do*not*depend on*N*. (One-time) Setup Communication.Setup communication is the material sent once before any query. The non-root shrink/expand setup consists of the offlineShrinkExpand*R*materials with

*|*ctr*|*=*O*(*µτ*) and*|*ctlac*|*=*O*(*µτ*log*µ*)

ring elements, and hence costs*O*(*µτ*log*µ|Rq|*) bits. This cost does not scale with*ℓ*: internal nodes in our recursion tree use the sameLHEkeysk(see Section 4.2), so one (ctlac*,*ctr) state supports every internal level. Adding the cost of the randomized-root wrapper with randomizer width*m*rt, the setup cost is:

*O αρτ²* log(*αρτ*)*·|Rq|*+ (*ρτ²* +*τm*rt) log(*ρτ*+*m*rt)*·|Rq| .*

For fixed*α,ρ,τ,m*rt, this setup cost is*O*(*|Rq|*) =*O*(*λ*(*m*msg+ log*N*)) bits.

Per-query Communication.For each query, the parties only need to exchange the randomized root digest and the root key. In the interactive mode,Rsends*d* *′* rt*∈Rq*, andSreplies withskrt *′* *∈Rq*and a*λ*-bit golden seed, for 2*|Rq|*+*λ*=*O*(*|Rq|*) =*O*(*λ*(*m*msg+ log*N*) 2 ) bits. For the non-interactive mode,Sneed not send skrt *′*, halving the communication cost. (One-time) Setup Computation.We count computation inMult*R*, since ring multiplications are the dominant operations. The setup cost comes from generating the two sets ofShrinkExpandmaterial (one for the leaf level, one for the internal levels). It costs*O*(*µτ*log*µ*) ring operations. In addition, the root- randomization wrapper contributes the same fixed-width setup term as in the communication count, giving the total cost *O αρτ²* log(*αρτ*) + (*ρτ²* +*τm*rt) log(*ρτ*+*m*rt) Mult*R.*

Per-query Computation.When a query walks down the recursion tree, it incurs*w*+*w/α*+*w/α²* +*···*= *O*(*w*) total inputs toShrinkExpandinstances. Moreover, theLacEevaluation for each ring element follows a path of length log*µ*, and each path step has*τ*gadget digits. Thus, it touches*O*(*wτ*log*µ*) ring elements. Observe that we can reuse the NTT representation to save computation: Sincectlacis fixed, the parties can store it in the NTT form, so the query can reuse that representation. Notice that a fullMult*R*includes the expensive NTT/INTT transforms, whereasMult NTT *Rq*only requires element-wise multiplication with NTT reuse. In our parameter regime,*τ*log*µ <*log*n*, so these*O*(*τ*log*µ*)Mult NTT *Rq*operations are cheaper than an ordinaryMult*R*(with*O*(log*n*) overhead). In addition to theShrinkExpandevaluation, the remaining LHE/LacEevaluations, denoising, and CRT/gadget recombination from Sections 6.1 and 6.2 contribute another*O*(*w*)Mult*R*, giving the query cost

*O*(*w*)Mult*R*+*O*(*wτ*log*µ*)Mult NTT *Rq.*

We keep theMult NTT *Rq*term explicit for concrete accounting, but under*τ*log*µ <*log*n*it is absorbed by the *O*(*w*)Mult*R*term. Thus the per-query cost is*O*(*N/n*) ring operations.

### 12.2 Evaluation

Parameter Setting.Across all experiments, we use the same concrete instantiation ofLogVoleand vary only the system resource being studied. We target*κ*= 128 computational security and*σ*= 40 statistical security. We implement our semi-honest protocol with one-digit gadget truncation and golden-seed optimiza- tions and the parameters below. (The malicious variant in Section 10.1.1 disables golden-seed grinding and is estimated to incur about a 2*∼*8*×*computation and communication overhead.) We pick plaintext moduli with log₂ *p*= 55 bits and set the ciphertext modulus to*ρ*= 4 primes with log₂ *qi*= 55 bits each, giving log₂ *q*= 220 bits. We use*Rq*of degree*n*= 8192, recursion constants*α*= 2 and*τ*= 2, and gadget*g*= 2 110, giving*µ*= 8. In the following experiments, we benchmark the parties generating*N*= 2 25 Z*p*-labels. Implementation Details.We implementedLogVoleinC++based onTinyLabel[DLL25]’s implementation and the Microsoft SEAL library [Mic23] for the underlying ring operations. Experimental Setup.We ran our experiments on ac8i.8xlargeinstance, which provides 16 physical Intel Xeon CPU cores of 3*.*9 GHz and 64 GiB of RAM, costing US$1*.*499 per hour. We enable Intel HEXL (which utilizes Intel AVX512 instruction set) on the machine to improve performance of the underlying ring operations. Communication Costs.For our parameter setting,LogVoleincurs a one-time setup communication cost of *≈*38 MB. For per-query communication, the parties invoke a ping-pong protocol to obtain*d* *′* rt*,*skrt *′* *∈Rq*and an insignificant*λ*-bit seed, which together cost about 451 KB; the two ring elements require 2*·n·*log₂ *q*= 2*·*2 13 *·*220*≈*451 KB. At the expense of disablingS’s precomputation, we can further reduce this to *n·*log₂ *q≈*226 KB using a trick inspired by our non-interactive variant⁹. Z*p*-Label Throughput.For the throughput experiments, we scale the number of threads per party from 1 to 16. Figure 11 reports the computation throughput for generatingZ*p*-labels usingLogVole. The online throughput scales from about 1*.*4 million labels/s on a single thread to about 12*.*9 million labels/s at 16 threads per party. When we include setup and sender pre-computation, the end-to-end throughput grows

The non-interactive variant in Section 4.4 can halve this query communication to*≈*226 KB: both parties deriveskrt *′* from (id*,d′*rt), soSneed not sendskrt *′*. This rules outS’s pre-computation, however, becausesk*k*is known only at query time.

from about 1*.*1 to 9*.*5 million labels/s. Because the setup is reusable, its amortized share stays small, ranging from about 1*.*3% to 7*.*8% across the measured thread counts. The sender pre-computation accounts for only about 17*.*7%–21*.*3%, and it can be overlapped with part ofR’s localShrinkcomputation to further reduce its effect on the critical path. ZK Multiplication Throughput.Figure 12 reports IT-MAC-based ZK multiplication-gate through- put for the opened protocol in Figure 18, instantiated withLogVoleas theCI-VOLElayer. Following JesseQ [LHLA25], the state-of-the-art IT-MAC-based ZK approach, we use a multiplication-chain circuit. The circuit proves that an array*A*satisfies*A*[*i*] = (*A*[*i−*2] +*A*[*i−*1])*·A*[*i−*1] mod*p*. This serial circuit is difficult to optimize using circuit structure, so it serves as a standard benchmark for raw ZK multiplication throughput. 10 Our online throughput increases from about 1*.*3 to 9*.*0 million multiplication gates/s as the number of threads grows from 1 to 16. The stacked bars decompose the computation time into the portion inherited fromZ*p*-label generation byLogVoleand the processing of VOLE-ZK. As the thread count in- creases,LogVoleaccounts for a smaller share of the runtime, while the VOLE-ZK processing becomes more visible because the multiplication chain has little parallelism to exploit. This indicates that, even for serial circuits,LogVole’s computation overhead is not the dominant cost. Performance Gain with Various Bandwidth.To show howLogVoleimproves ZK performance across bandwidth regimes, Figure 13 compares theLogVole-based ZK scheme against an IT-MAC-based ZK scheme that derandomizes VOLE tuples. To favor derandomization approaches, we only count their communication

We do not separately benchmark addition gates because addition is free: it requires no additional labels, and its consistency proof consists of local additions over labels.

time but*ignore their computation time*. We assume their communication cost is*N·*log₂ *p*, where*N*is the number of multiplication gates and log₂ *p*is the label size, which matches the costs in [YSWW21] and [LHLA25] 11. Here, we assume*N*= 2 25. For our approach, we assume a computation throughput of 9 million gates/s as in our multiplication-chain benchmark. Even under this conservative comparison,LogVoleyields about 45*×*projected speedup at 10 Mbps and 4*.*9*×*at 100 Mbps, remains above 1*×*through 256 Mbps, and falls slightly below parity at 512 Mbps. This matches the intended use case ofLogVole: when bandwidth is the bottleneck, reducing the online communication from linear to essentially constant gives a substantial concrete advantage. Matrix Multiplication.Table 4 reports the matrix-multiplication benchmark commonly used in the IT-MAC-based ZKP literature. Because prior IT-MAC-based work uses a 61-bit prime while our plaintext modulus is 55 bits, we scale the prior communication costs by 55*/*61 to estimate their cost over the same-size field. The results show that our runtime is faster than both the zk-SNARK and IT-MAC-based systems, and our communication is the lowest among the IT-MAC-based systems. As in JesseQ and other prior works, we benchmark the online runtime, and our proof checks the*n²* inner products for multiplying two*n×n* matrices. 12

# Supplementary Material

## A Digest Properties

### A.1 Roadmap

This section isolates the digest-side facts used later by both security arguments. The digest enters the protocol in two different ways. First, the receiver reveals one randomized public root digest. Second, the translate/expand algebra uses the lifted opening input below that digest. Accordingly, the security proof needs three properties from the digest layer. The first is a*root hiding*statement showing that the randomized root node can be revealed without leaking the receiver’s input. The second is a*binding*statement showing that bounded openings are computationally unique. The third is a*smudging*statement showing that large openings are statistically useless in thectlac-row evaluations. These properties are used in different places. The semi-honest proof cites the randomized-root hid- ing lemma from this section, and then carries out a TinyLabel-style recursive simulation. The malicious proof cites the bounded-opening uniqueness theorems and the smudging lemma, and then adds a separate extraction argument.

### A.2 Hiding of the randomized root digest

The lower-level realization in Section 6.4 introduces a special randomized node at the root of the digest tree:

RandNodeDigest *τ,m* *B′* (*u,ζ*) :=*B₁ ·*g *−*1

Here we record the digest-security fact: for a slightly wider randomizer dimension*m*, this published root is statistically close to uniform. The intuition is the Ajtai-commitment viewpoint also used in laconic encryption [DKL + 23b]: the digest is an affine map with a message part and a randomizer part. Here the message part is*B₁ ·*g *−*1

(*u*), while

## B Formalization and Semi-honest Security

In this section, we prove semi-honest security for theCI-VOLEconstruction built from theZ*p*-level wrapper LogLabelZand the ring-level core protocolLogLabel*R*. The wrapperLogLabelZfrom Figure 3 reducesCI-VOLE overZ*p*toCI-VOLEover*Rq*, and the core ring protocolLogLabel*R*is described in Figures 9 and 10. Our focus here is the core semi-honest argument for the construction from Sections 4 and 6, excluding the malicious-security analysis in Section 10 and the optimizations in Section 7.

### B.1 Correctness

Interface to the generalized functionality.LogLabelZand LogLabel*R*do not expose separate public methods namedset-delta,setx-ni,release-ni,setk-int,setx-intor release-int. Instead, the functionality interface from Figure 6 is realized through the two protocol entry points ofLogLabelZ. The callLogLabelZ*.*Offline(S: ∆*,*B: *w*) realizes*F*CI-VOLE*.*set-delta(S: ∆*,w*). The online callLogLabelZ*.*Online(B:id= (sid*,*mode)) is then interpreted according to the mode tag. In the interactive

mode, one online invocation on (sid*,*x) follows the timing ofsetk-int,setx-int, andrelease-int: the sender- side share is fixed before the receiver output is released, and the final output pair again satisfies the same VOLE relation. In the non-interactive mode, one online invocation on receiver input (sid*,*x) computes the pair (k*,*m) corresponding to the functionality entries created bysetx-niand later released byrelease-ni; the protocol returns both shares locally, even though the functionality conceptually delays the sender’s output until release.

Theorem 8(Correctness of the coreCI-VOLEprotocol).*Let*Π*be theCI-VOLEprotocol obtained by* *combining the wrapperLogLabel*Z*from Figure 3 with the core ring protocolLogLabelRfrom Figures 9* *and 10, excluding the optimization from Section 7. Then*Π*realizesFCI-VOLEin both the interactive and* *non-interactive settings of Figure 6. Concretely, forw≤*2 *λ* *, every session identifier*sid*, every mode* mode*∈{interactive,non-interactive}, and every*∆*∈*Z*p,*x*∈*Z *w* *p,*

Pr[(k*,*m)*←*Π(S: ∆*,*R: (sid*,*x)*,*B:mode) :m=x*·*∆ +k] = 1*.*

*Proof sketch.*The detailed proof first treats the interactive mode. It starts from the actual inputx, writes the encoded ring input asEncode(x), and then describes the recursive input vector processed at each level. The base case is the root level, whereDerandRootconverts the short relation for the randomized root digest into the short relation for the ordinary root digest. From that point, the proof uses a per-level noisy expand lemma and induction on the recursion levels: if the incoming short relation at one level is correct, then the local expand step introduces only bounded LacE, LHE, and masking noise, and the denoising margin removes that noise exactly, so the caller recovers the correct short relation for the next level. Iterating this argument reaches the encoded leaf input, where the leaf denoising step yields the exact ring-level VOLE relation, andEncode *−*1 then recovers the finalZ*p*-level output relation. The non-interactive mode follows immediately, because after it establishes the same randomized-root short relation, the rest of the argument is identical. See Section B.3 for the full proof.

Theorem 9(Semi-honest security of the core protocol).*Assume the*LacE*and*LHE*instantiations used* *by the protocol are*2 *λ* *-simulation secure with the leakage stated in Sections 3.6.2 and 3.6.4, and assume* *the randomized root digest is statistically hiding as in Lemma 7. Let*Π*be theCI-VOLEprotocol obtained* *by combining the wrapperLogLabel*Z*from Figure 3 with the core ring protocolLogLabelRfrom Figures 9* *and 10, excluding the optimization from Section 7.*Π*is semi-honestly secure against corrupted senders and* *receivers (Def. 11 and 12).*

*Proof sketch.*For a corruptedS, the simulator reproduces the offline view exactly, replaces the randomized root digest by a uniform ring element using Lemma 7 and theorem 4, and then programs the session-specific *H*- and*H*ct-queries so that the sender’s local computation is consistent with the released sender output in both modes. A short hybrid argument shows that the only changes are the digest replacement, the programmed correction ciphertexts, and a negligible pre-query abort event. For a corruptedR, the simulator first replaces the receiver-visible setup ciphertexts, then programs the online oracle points from the leaves upward, and finally simulates the root response. At each level, the next target relation is obtained from the previous one by the same deterministic recombination map used in the protocol. The corresponding hybrid argument first programs the random oracle with valid ciphertexts and then replaces the level-*t*LacEandLHEsetup ciphertexts, together with the corresponding correction ciphertexts (used in programming the random oracle), by their simulated counterparts, first at the leaf level, then at each intermediate level, and finally at the root. The full proof appears in Appendix B.4.

### B.4 Proof of Theorem 9 (Semi-honest Security of the Core Protocol)

*Proof.*Below, we treat the two-corruption cases separately. The corrupt-sender case proves Definition 11, and the corrupt-receiver case is the analogous simulation statement on the receiver side. Together they imply the theorem. Case 1:Sis corrupt.We first isolate the parts of the corrupted sender’s view whose simulation is not immediate. In the offline phase, the corruptedSsees only its input (∆*,w*), its random tape, and the sender state produced by the local computation. It receives no message fromRinLogLabelZ*.*Offline,LogVole*R.*Offline, ShrinkExpand*R.*Offline, or SetupRoot. Hence, the simulation of the offline view is straight-forward. In the online phase, in both the interactive and non-interactive mode, the only message received bySis the randomized root digest*d* *′* rt. By Lemma 7 and theorem 4, this digest is statistically close to a uniform element of*Rq*. AfterSreceives*d* *′* rt, the rest of its view is determined by its tape, its offline state, and the oracle interfaces*H*and*H*ct. The sender side oracle calls are: •FindGoldenSeed(id*,k*rt *′* *,d* *′* rt) in the interactive mode; •*H*(id*,d* *′* rt) in the non-interactive mode; •*H*ct(id*,ς,*rt*,*0) insideDerandRoot; •*H*ct(id*,ς,j,i*) inside every call toShrinkExpand*R.*Expand. The ideal output is the vectork, released atsetk-intin the interactive mode and atrelease-niin the non- interactive mode. Thus the simulation task is to replace*d* *′* rtby a uniform ring element and to program*H* and*H*ctso thatS’s local computation remains consistent with the releasedk.

On a fresh query, it records and returns a fresh uniform answer of the appropriate type. Both the corruptedSand the simulator may query these tables.

Equivalently, the corresponding bound test*∥*e*j,i∥∞≤* *B*fails. By the golden-seed analysis in Section 7, acceptance occurs with constant probability, so the simulator can reproduce the same rejection pattern with constant expected overhead.

Hybrid argument for corruptS.We now prove that, for the simulatorSim above,Game is com- putationally indistinguishable fromGame. Let*H* denote the*i*-th hybrid, and let*D* denote the joint distribution of the corruptedS-view and the honestR-output whenSimSruns*H*.

•*H₂*: this hybrid is the same as*H₁*, except that in each session it samples the target vectorkuniformly at random and then programs the oracle points exactly as in the interactive and non-interactive branches of SimS. We compare the two views directly. In the interactive mode,SimSfirst emulatesFindGoldenSeedby programming the candidate*H*-answers so that rejected counters produce the same bad-noise event used in the real bound test, and the first accepting counter produces the same success event. Since the real procedure also scans counters until the first seed whose designated correction ciphertexts satisfy the same bound, this emulation distribution is unchanged. Conditioned on an accepting seed, each programmed point*Q*ct[(sid*,ς,*rt*,*0)] and*Q*ct[(sid*,ς,t,i*)] is sampled so that decryption under the known sender key returns scaled target plaintext plus a noise vector drawn coefficient-wise from the same good interval as in the real execution. Thus, from the corruptedS’s perspective, the programmed oracle answers have the same distribution as the honest correction ciphertexts conditioned on seed acceptance. The same argument applies in the non-interactive mode, except that the accepting seed is fixed directly by*QH*[(sid*, d*b *′* rt)] rather than by a counter search. Finally, the simulation aborts only if a point that must be programmed has already been fixed in*QH*or*Q*ct. This is a bad event consisting of a pre-query to one of the session-specific programmed points, and its probability is negligible by the polynomial query bound and the randomness of*ς*. Hence *D₂ ≈cD₁.*

•*H₃*: this hybrid is the same as*H₂*, except that the randomized root digest*d* *′* rtis replaced by a uniform element of*Rq*. By Lemma 7 and theorem 4,

Case 2:Ris corrupt. We first isolate the parts of the corrupted receiver’s view whose simulation is not immediate. In the offline phase, the corruptedRholds the public width*w*and receives three sender-generated ciphertext collections: •the leaf ciphertexts (ctlac*,*leaf*,*ctr*,*leaf) from the call toShrinkExpand*R.*Offline on the leaf input vector in LogVole*R.*Offline; •when*ℓ >*1, the internal ciphertexts (ctlac*,*int*,*ctr*,*int) from the call toShrinkExpand*R.*Offline on the internal recursion input inLogVole*R.*Offline; •the root ciphertexts (ctlac*,*rt*,*ctr*,*rt*,B* *∗* ) from SetupRoot.

We simulate the threeLacE-ciphertexts usingLacE*.*Sim₁ from Section 3.6.2, and we simulate the three LHE*.*Enc₁-ciphertexts usingLHE*.*Sim₁ from Section 3.6.4. No other offline message fromSdepends onR’s future input. In the online phase, the relevant calls are (setx-int*,*release-int) in the interactive mode andsetx-niin the non-interactive mode. The simulator receives the corruptedR’s inputxfrom the call sequence and obtains the corresponding ideal outputmfrom the ideal functionality. The seed*ς*can be simulated by sampling a uniform element of*{*0*,*1*}* *λ*. The non-trivial step is to program the random-oracle ciphertexts so that the receiver-side decryptions are consistent with the target outputm. Informally, the simulator works from the leaf level upward. It first encodesm, uses the leafLacE- andLHE-simulators to manufacture receiver-side decryption targets for the leaf blocks, programs the corresponding*H*ct-values, and then uses the resulting simulated key vector as the target input for the next recursion level. Repeating this step level by level eventually reaches the root. The root response is then sent directly in the interactive mode and is installed as the output of the online random-oracle query in the non-interactive mode.

Hybrid argument for corruptR.We now prove that, for the simulatorSimRabove,Game is compu- tationally indistinguishable fromGame. For each hybrid*H* below, let

#### D := (view,out)

denote the joint distribution of the corruptedR-view and the honestS-output. •*H₀*: the honestSruns Π on input ∆, and the corruptedRinteracts with it in the real execution. Thus

*D₀ ≡*Game real

R*.*
•*H₁*: this hybrid is the same as*H₀*, except that all answers to*H*and*H*ctare generated lazily from the tables*QH*and*Q*ct. Since the real execution samples the same answers independently and reuses them on repeated queries, *D₁ ≡D₀.*

•*H₂*: this hybrid is the same as*H₁*, except that it programs the session-specific oracle points exactly as described bySimR, but using the actual ciphertexts. In the interactive mode, the hybrid emulates the same seed-selection rule as the real execution: rejected seeds are precisely those for which some programmed correction ciphertext produces a bad-noise event, and the first accepted seed is one for which every relevant ciphertext lies in the good interval. In the non-interactive mode, the same condition is enforced directly on the programmed seed. Since the programmed correction ciphertexts have the same ciphertext-plus- good-noise form as in the real execution, the resulting joint distribution is unchanged except for the abort event that some programmed point has already been queried. This pre-query event is negligible by the polynomial query bound and the randomness of*ς*. Hence

#### D₂ ≈cD₁.

•*H₃*: this hybrid is the same as*H₂*, except that at the leaf level it samples the encoded leaf targetm *′ℓ*

uniformly at random and programs the*H*ct-points with the leaf nonces (sid*,ς,ℓ,i*) so that receiver-side denoising returns these chosen leaf blocks. This preserves the joint distribution, because at the leaf level the clean relation is affine: choosing the sender blocksk*ℓ*uniformly or choosing the receiver blocksm *′ℓ*

uniformly determines the other side by the same affine relation. Thus

#### D₃ ≡D₂.

•*H₄*: this hybrid is the same as*H₃*, except that it replaces the leaf-levelLacE(resp.LHE) setup ciphertext by the output ofLacE*.*Sim₁ (resp.LHE*.*Sim₁), replaces the leaf-levelLHEcorrection ciphertexts by the outputs ofLHE*.*Sim₂, programs the leaf-level*H*ct-points as inSimR, and then defines the next recursion target*m* (*ℓ−*1) from the leafLHE*.*Sim₃ outputs exactly as inSimR. Adaptive security ofLacEandLHE implies *D₄ ≈cD₃.*

•*H₅*: for each intermediate level*t*=*ℓ−*1*,...,*2, this hybrid replaces the level-*t*LacEsetup ciphertext byLacE*.*Sim₁, replaces the rootLHEsetup ciphertext byLHE*.*Sim₁, replaces the level-*t*LHEcorrection ciphertexts byLHE*.*Sim₂, programs the corresponding*H*ct-points as inSimR, and uses theLHE*.*Sim₃- outputs to define the next target vector. Adaptive security ofLacEandLHEat each level implies

#### D₅ ≈cD₄.

•*H₆*: this hybrid is the same as*H₅*, except that it replaces the rootLacEsetup ciphertext byLacE*.*Sim₁, replaces the rootLHEsetup ciphertext byLHE*.*Sim₁, replaces the rootLHEcorrection ciphertexts by the outputs ofLHE*.*Sim₂, programs the root oracle points as inSimR, and obtains the root response from LHE*.*Sim₃. Adaptive security of the rootLacEandLHEsetup objects implies

#### D₆ ≈cD₅.

## D Security Analysis for Truncation Optimization

Security concerns about the*d·r*term.For a local LacE block with digest*d*=*d*x=*Aw*, the telescoping identity in Section 6 gives, in the full-gadget case,

LacE*.*Eval(ctlac*,*x)[ind]*≈*r[ind]*·d−*s[ind]*·*x[ind]*.*

The truncated version has the same leading term. More precisely, by Equation (17),

LacEhi*.*Eval(ctlachi*,*x)[ind] =r[ind]*·d−*s[ind]*·*x[ind] +*e*drop(ind) +*e*LacE(ind)*,*

where*e*dropis the known dropped-digit contribution and*e*LacEis the usual LacE evaluation noise. Thus the digest-indexed correction material for digest*d*is designed to cancel the top termr[ind]*·d*. Now suppose the receiver keeps the correction/key material associated with*d*=*Aw*, but evaluates a second bounded opening*w* *′* with digest*d* *′* =*Aw* *′*. Suppressing the already-budgeted truncation and Gaussian errors, letxandx *′* denote the leaf values induced by*w*and*w* *′*, respectively. The two secret- dependent message terms are, explicitly,s[ind]*·*x[ind] for the intended leaf ands[ind]*·*x *′* [ind] for the substituted leaf. If k˜*d*[ind] denotes the fixed local sender share/key offset supplied by the surrounding correction layer, then the intended opening gives the local receiver share

#### m˜d,x[ind] := k˜d[ind] +r[ind]·d − r[ind]·d−s[ind]·x[ind]

#### = k˜d[ind] +s[ind]·x[ind].

#### The second opening gives instead

m˜ sub *d,*x*′*[ind] := k ˜ *d* [ind] +r[ind]*·d −* r[ind]*·d* *′* *−*s[ind]*·*x *′* [ind]

= k˜*d*[ind] +s[ind]*·*x *′* [ind] +r[ind]*·*(*d−d* *′* )*.*

Therefore an exact digest collision is stronger than necessary for an attack: a near collision can also be useful if the extra termr[ind]*·*(*d−d* *′* ) stays inside the rounding/noise budget, since the receiver then obtains the same form of local message term k˜*d*[ind] +s[ind]*·*x *′* [ind], but under the reused digest-*d*sender offset. The consequence is that the digest-indexed correction for one opening can be reused as if it were a correction for another opening. Comparing the intended opening with the second one gives

m˜ sub *d,*x*′*[ind]*−* m˜*d,*x[ind] =s[ind]*·*(x *′* [ind]*−*x[ind]) +r[ind]*·*(*d−d* *′* )*.*

If the near-collision term is small enough to be absorbed by the decoding margin, the receiver learns a noisy linear equation in the sender secrets[ind]. Concretely, write

*a*ind:=x *′* [ind]*−*x[ind]*, η*ind:=r[ind]*·*(*d−d* *′* )

after folding the already-budgeted LacE/LHE and dropped-digit errors into*η*ind. The observed difference has the form ∆*m*ind:= m˜ sub *d,*x*′*[ind]*−* m˜*d,*x[ind] =s[ind]*·a*ind+*η*ind*.* If*η*indlies within half of the relevant rounding gap, then denoising removes it exactly:

#### Denoise(ϕ,∆mind) =Denoise(ϕ,s[ind]·aind).

¯ ind *s* =*⌈*∆*m*ind*⌋⌈p/q⌋·*¯*a* *−* ind1 (mod*p*)*,*

where ¯ind*s* denotes the decoded sender secret in that slot. With the opposite subtraction order one uses the opposite denominator, so the sign is immaterial. At recursive levels the same calculation is applied componentwise with the corresponding CRT scale factor*qt/q*: one denoises the difference to obtains[ind]*·a*ind modulo the component, and then inverts the chosen unit component of*a*ind. More generally, several near- colliding openings yield equations

(*t*) (*t*)

in the decoded domain; if the chosen differences have full rank, solving this linear system revealss[ind]. This breaks the sender-secret privacy that the LacE/LHE correction is supposed to protect.

Proof strategy.The truncation-specific binding issue is that a malicious receiver does not need an exact second opening of the digest. It may try to find two bounded openings*w,w* *′* with digest values*d*=*Aw*and *d* *′* =*Aw* *′* such that the short-mask discrepancy

(*d−d* *′* )*·r*

is still small enough to pass through rounding and preserve the message. If*d−d* *′* is large, multiplication by the Gaussian mask*r← D*smudges the discrepancy. By Lemma 8, (*d−d* *′* )*·r*has at most exp(*−π*)-scale distinguishing advantage from uniform once*∥d−d* *′* *∥* 2*≥q/s*mask. The concrete security calculation below accounts for the cost of finding such short dual vectors. If*d−d* *′* is small enough to avoid smudging, then *z*:=*w−w* *′* gives an approximate SIS relation

*Az*= 0 +*e*(mod*q*)*, e*:=*d−d* *′* *,*

with both*z*and*e*short. Thus every useful second opening either loses its signal by smudging or yields a short approximate SIS witness, which we embed into normal-form SIS for [*A|−*1].

Useful near-collision premise.The attack specific to the truncated protocol is not an exact digest collision. It is a near collision that leaves the masking term small. If a receiver uses a digest-indexed key for *d*=*Aw*while evaluating another bounded opening*w* *′*, then the mismatch contains

(*d−d* *′* )*·r, d* *′* :=*Aw* *′* *,*

where*r← D*is one of the short masks used in the truncated rows. Let*s*maskbe the Gaussian parameter of these masks and define the smudging radius <u>q</u> *α*use:=*.*(22) *s* mask This is the digest-difference analogue of the bounded-opening threshold*β*from Corollary 1: it marks the point beyond which the relevant product with fresh Gaussian noise has advantage at most exp(*−π*). The radius is not a correctness margin; it separates openings lost to smudging from openings that must be ruled out computationally.

Lemma 12(Digest-difference smudging).*Letd*=*Awandd* *′* =*Aw* *′* *be two digest values for a selected* *truncated row, and letr← Dbe the corresponding mask. If∥d−d* *′* *∥* 2*> α*use*, then*(*d−d* *′* )*·rhas at most* exp(*−π*)*-scale distinguishing advantage from uniform, up to the statistical distance between the untruncated* *and truncated Gaussian experiments.*

Replacing the untruncated Gaussian by its truncated version changes the experiment only by the statistical distance in Lemma 2. Such an opening cannot preserve the message.

Thus the only collisions that can remain useful are bounded openings whose digest difference has norm at most*α*use. Concrete Security Evaluation.We now instantiate the useful near-collision premise for the parameter set used in Section 12. There we take*λ*= 128,*n*= 8192 = 2 13, log₂ *q*= 220. This parameter set conforms to the HE standard [ACC + 19], which shows that it achieves 130 bits of security against an adversary solving *√* the SIS problem. The near-collision search problem relaxes the SIS bound by a factor of 5, which slightly reduces the estimated cost to*≈*128 bits. The detailed security estimate follows. We first set the useful radius for near-collision vectors. This is the per-sample distinguishing scale targeted by the dual attack in the HE standard [ACC + 19], not a negligible-bias threshold. The Gaussian parameter is*s*= 8, in the convention where the ordinary standard deviation is

<u>s</u> *σ*= *√ ≈*3*.*2*.* 2*π*

The dual-attack formula uses the LWE noise rate *√* <u>s 2πσ</u> *α*HES= =*.* *q q*

For a dual relation vector*v*, the distinguishing advantage is estimated as [ACC + 19, Section 2.1.2.c]

exp *−π*(*α*HES*∥v∥*2) 2 *.*

At*∥v∥*2=*q/s*, this advantage is*e* *−π*. This is the per-sample distinguishing advantage from one short dual vector, not the final security advantage. The HE-standard tables normalize attack costs to a fixed success probability, namely 51% [ACC + 19, Section 2.1.2.e]; the BKZ work and repetitions needed to amplify the per-sample advantage to that probability are included in the reported cost. Therefore the near-collision check uses the same per-sample scale and then estimates the cost of finding such a relation under the HES BKZ.sieve model: 1 *q q* *α*HES*∥v∥*2*≈*1*,∥v∥*2*≈* = = *√.* *α*HES*s* 2*πσ*

Therefore the per-sample distinguishing radius is

We use the slightly more conservative rounded value

We instantiate Lemma 13 with*β*=*α*= Γnc. The augmented normal-form SIS witness has two opening- difference coordinates and one digest-error coordinate, so its Euclidean target radius is p *√* *B*near(Γnc*,*Γnc) = (2Γnc)2+ Γ²nc= 5 Γnc= 0*.*254911749435*q.*

#### Since log₂ q= 220, this gives

log₂ *B*near= 220 + log₂(0*.*254911749435) = 218*.*028069777*.*

We now compute the cost of finding such a vector using the BKZ.sieve model as in [ACC + 19, Sections 2.1.2 and 2.1.5]. The augmented row [Emb(*A*)*|−In*] has determinant*q* *n* and ambient dimension 3*n*. For a dropped lattice dimension*d∈*[*n*+ 1*,*3*n*], the GSA prediction is

<u>nlog₂ q</u> log₂ *∥v∥*2*≈d*log₂ *δ₀*(*b*) +*,* *d*

where the Chen root-Hermite factor used in [ACC + 19] is

<u>log (b/(2πe)) + log (πb)/b</u> log₂ *δ₀*(*b*) =*.* 2(*b−*1)

For each*d*, we solve for the smallest block size*b*that reaches the target norm, then evaluate the HES sieve cost log₂ Costsieve(*b,d*) = 0*.*292*b*+ 16*.*4 + log₂(8*d*)*.*

Minimizing over*d∈*[8193*,*24576] and rounding the BKZ block size upward, the optimum is*d*= 16294 and *b*= 326. The resulting security exponent is therefore

*κ*near= 0*.*292*·*326 + 16*.*4 + log₂(8*·*16294) = 128*.*584053193*.*

Here*κ*nearis the usual “security bits” value: the estimated attack cost is 2 *κ* nearelementary operations under the HES BKZ.sieve model. Thus the concrete augmented-SIS near-collision search is above the 128-bit target.

The preceding lemma is a fixed-query hiding statement, exactly as inTinyLabel’s proof of LEnc: the databases are fixed inputs to the simulator, and possible digest collisions among those databases are not ruled out or needed. The changer*,*r

The near-collision premise is a different statement: it says that one digest-indexed decryption key cannot be useful for two distinct bounded openings whose digest difference remains below the smudging radius. That premise is therefore used in the receiver-view hybrids of Theorem 10, before applying the fixed-queryLacEhisimulator.

### D.4 Composed truncated protocol

Theorem 10(Semi-honest security of the truncated protocol).*Assume the premises of Lemmas 14 and 15,* *the root-digest hiding condition of Lemma 7, the normal-form Ring-SIS hardness required by Lemma 13, and* *the truncated correctness relation Equation*(17)*under the rounding condition of Equation*(21)*. Then*Πhi *securely realizesFCI-VOLEin the random-oracle model against static semi-honest corruptions.*

*Proof.*We prove the two corruption cases separately. In both cases the simulator uses a lazy random-oracle table*Q*: a fresh, non-programmed query receives a uniform ciphertext-shaped answer, while programmed digest-indexed queries receive the ciphertext specified by the simulator.

Corrupt sender.The simulatorSim hi Sfollows the sender-side simulator from Section B.3, with every full- gadget object replaced by its truncated counterpart. It samples the sender’s ideal output share, samples the root digest as *d*b hi rt*←*high*g*(*URq*), and programs the designated oracle points indexed by *d* b hi rtso that the recursiveLHEhidecryptions produce the target sender share at the leaves.

|andD|be the real and ideal distributions for the corrupt sender. Define hybrids:||||||||
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|S||||hi|R S|S|||
|S||||||hi rt|g R||
|||||g|||||
|S|||||||||
|||||S|||||

Let*DR* *S* *IS* *HybridH₀* *S* *.*This is the real execution of Πhi, so*DR* *S* *≡D₀* *S*. *HybridH₁.*Replace the real randomized root digest by *d*b *←*high (*Uq*). This is statistically close to *H₀* *S* by Lemma 7 and post-processing byhigh. *HybridH₂.*Answer the random oracle lazily from*Q*, except for designated points that will later be pro- grammed. This is identically distributed to*H₁* until a programmed point is queried before it is programmed;

that event has probability at most the number of designated points divided by the ciphertext space size, hence negligible. *HybridH₃* *S* *.*Program the designated points with simulatedLHEhicorrection ciphertexts and matching keys, level by level, using the residual plaintexts dictated by the recursive expand equations. For each level and block, the replacement of the real bridge/correction view by the simulated one is justified by Lemma 14. Since the number of levels and blocks is polynomial,*D₂* *S* *≈cD₃* *S*. *HybridH₄* *S* *.*Replace the honest receiver’s output by the output of*F*CI-VOLEusing the sender share selected by the simulator. Equations (17) and (21) show that the programmed truncated execution decodes the same CI-VOLE relation, including the dropped low-digit terms, so*D₃* *S* *≈cD₄* *S*. The last hybrid is exactly the ideal execution, and therefore*DR* *S* *≈cDIS*.

Corrupt receiver.The simulatorSim hi Ris given the receiver’s inputxand ideal outputm. It computes all truncated digest values honestly fromx, runs the recursive partitioning used by the protocol, and simulates each local block as follows. First it invokesLacEhi*.*Simon the block query vectors to obtain the laconic ciphertext and fake evaluation errors. It then samples the hiding noise from *χ*LacE, sets the desired residual plaintext for the localLHEhicall so that subtracting the simulated LacE evaluation yields the target noisy ex- pand identity, and invokesLHEhi*.*Sim. The resulting correction ciphertexts are installed at the corresponding digest-indexed oracle points. Let*DR* *R* and*DIR*be the real and ideal distributions for the corrupt receiver, augmented with the out- put of an arbitrary polynomial-time local post-processing algorithm applied to the receiver’s view. This augmentation is without loss of generality, since indistinguishability is closed under efficient post-processing. *HybridH₀* *R* *.*This is the real execution, so*DR* *R* *≡D₀* *R*. *HybridH₁* *R* *.*Replace the random oracle by the lazy table*Q*. Equations (17) and (21) guarantee that each level denoises back to the same clean recursive interface used by the simulator, so the residual plaintexts given to the LHE simulator are consistent across levels. The final hybrid is therefore distributed exactly as the ideal execution with receiver inputxand output m, except for the negligible hybrid gaps and the negligibleBaduseevent. Thus*DR* *R* *≈cDIR*. Combining the corrupt-sender and corrupt-receiver cases completes the proof.

## E Proofs for Malicious Security

### E.1 Roadmap and the malicious interface

The malicious proof has three jobs. First, for a corrupted receiver, the simulator must identify the effective input*x*, simulate the honest-sender objects that the receiver sees, and program the random-oracle-derived correction so that the receiver’s final output is the ideal output. The receiver input can be recovered in two ways. The random-linear-check wrapper of Section 10.1.6 gives an explicit extraction mechanism, proved inline in the corrupted-receiver hybrids. Alternatively, Section E.4 gives a no-message knowledge extraction route for the public digest. Second, for a corrupted sender, the simulator must extract the sender’s effective correlation data, in particular the ∆-side setup encoded by the reusable ciphertext package, define the fresh online mask from the sender’s response, and then generate a receiver-facing transcript consistent with the ideal functionality. This is why the sender side is organized around an extractable setup proof; the malicious protocol does not use golden-seed grinding and has no separate sender-side online proof. Third, the single-identifier statements must lift to executions with many identifiers. The shared setup is handled once, while the online messages, random-oracle labels, and ideal-functionality table entries are in- dexed byid= (sid*,*mode). The final theorem therefore combines receiver extraction, sender-side certification, random-oracle programming, and a hybrid over identifiers.

Outgoing messages are not listed in the sender’s or receiver’s own view; they are determined when the party is rerun from its view, and appear instead as incoming messages in the other party’s view. ### E.4 Knowledge Extraction ofx

This section states the optional knowledge-extraction route for recovering the receiver’s effective input from the public digest. The malicious construction does not rely on this route exclusively: the random-linear- check adaptor of Section 10.1.6 gives an explicit alternative with the same asymptotic efficiency. The explicit adaptor adds a final check round in the interactive protocol; in the non-interactive protocol, the check challenge is derived from the same online random-oracle response, so the round structure is unchanged. We nevertheless include the knowledge route because it captures the apparent native security property of the digest layer. The malicious receiver does not only output a digest value; it subsequently uses that digest in the translation algebra. For this use to remain meaningful under the noise analysis, the relevant openings below the digest must be bounded. The assumption below formalizes the corresponding knowledge statement: whenever a digest is used successfully in this admissible bounded sense, an extractor can recover the bounded opening data that underlies that use. This is analogous in spirit to the random-oracle convention that a party using a hash value must have queried the oracle, but it is strictly narrower. We do not model Digestas a random oracle, and we do not assume that arbitrary digest outputs are invertible; the extraction target is only the bounded witness data that a successful receiver uses, explicitly or implicitly, inside this protocol.

E.4.1 Digest-knowledge extraction from the public root On the receiver side, the only object that enters the later translation algebra is the public digest*dx*. Any simulation argument that wants to recover the leaves of*x*, or the branch data used by the translate/expand identities from Section 5.3, can therefore try to extract that information from the digest layer itself. A generic proof that*dx*is a correct digest of a large vector*x*is not the right tool here. The receiver input is large, and the whole point of the digest layer is to compress it. For the receiver side, we instead isolate the narrower knowledge-style property actually consumed by the simulator: whenever a malicious receiver produces a digest that admits an admissible use in the translation algebra, the corresponding branch data can be extracted from the receiver’s computation. The digest section has already done the computational work needed for this route. Corollary 1 identifies the bounded regime in which an opening can still affect the translation algebra, and Theorems 5 and 6 say that within that regime the digest is computationally tied to at most one opening tree of the type used by the protocol. What remains here is the extraction step. The point is not that arbitrary outputs of a hash-like function should be generically extractable. Rather, the claim is that in the present protocol, the only way for a malicious receiver to produce a digest that is actually useful in the later algebra is to have implicitly determined the corresponding input data below that digest. The extractor is a non-black-box device for recovering that implicit information from the adversary’s computation. This is best viewed as a knowledge-style heuristic. Related work of Canetti and Dakdouk on extractable perfectly one-way functions [CD08], and of Bitansky, Canetti, Chiesa, and Tromer on extractable collision- resistant hashes [BCCT11], gives useful language and precedent for this type of statement. In particular, the

latter work explicitly studies extractability in settings built from Merkle-tree hashing. At the same time, those works do not directly prove the exact statement needed here, and we therefore state the required property directly as an assumption adapted to our protocol. The important point is that the assumption is about recovering the admissible opening below one digest node, not about privileging any one particular implementation path by which the adversary arrived at that digest. An adversary might start from explicit opening data and compute a digest, modify internal data and compute a related digest, or arrive at the same output by some more complicated computation. The pair need not ever appear as an explicit variable in the adversary’s state. The extractor is allowed to be non-black-box and to recover the admissible witness from the adversary’s internal computation rather than from a literal stored opening. To make the target explicit, let*T*denote the accepted translate/expand transcript around one digest node*d*. Write Usedig(*T,d*;*uL,uR*) = 1 if (*uL,uR*) is an admissible opening of*d*in the sense of Definition 9, and the row evaluations in*T*that use this node are explained by the lifted witness*w*(*uL,uR*) from Section A. This relation is a proof-level abstraction of the algebra in Section 5.3: it means that the adversary has produced messages that are useful precisely because they behave as if evaluated on a small witness. An out-of-bound formal preimage is not a successful use in this sense, since Corollary 1 says that its row evaluations are already smudged. Thus “*d* is later used successfully” will mean that there exists an ordered pair (*uL,uR*) withUsedig(*T,d*;*uL,uR*) = 1. By Theorem 5, such an admissible opening is computationally unique. This is the protocol-specific reason the assumption is plausible: the extractor is not being asked to invert an arbitrary stand-alone hash output, but to recover the concrete small witness that the adversary is already using, either explicitly or implicitly, to drive the later linear translation algebra. Accordingly, the extraction target used by the malicious proof is concrete. Fix one block of width*µ*, and let*ℓ*:= log₂ *µ*be the depth of the corresponding binary digest tree. The extractor recursively descends below the public root digest*dx*and attempts to recover the leaf values at depth*ℓ*. If a branch stops early, the extractor records a hole there. Thus the final extracted object is a partial leaf vector

ˆ*x∈*(*Rq∪{⊥}*) *µ* *.*

Here ˆ*xj∈Rq*means that the extractor recovered the*j*-th leaf value together with a full root-to-leaf branch consistent with the concrete digest relation, while ˆ*xj*=*⊥*means that the corresponding branch could not be fully resolved down to depth*ℓ*. It is convenient to package this as a partial tree*Tx*. The root of*Tx*is labeled by*dx*. Each extracted internal node is labeled by its ordered pair of children. A branch may terminate early, in which case no children are claimed below that node. Write

#### Rdig(dx;Tx) = 1

for this partial-tree consistency condition, and let

Remark.This assumption is stated for one binary digest node. At internal levels, the extracted pair (*uL,uR*) is interpreted as the two child digests below that node. At the bottom level, the extracted pair is interpreted as the two child leaf values. We extend the single-node extractor to a tree by starting at the public root*dx*, recursing on every extracted child digest, and stopping a branch whenever extraction fails. At depth*ℓ*, the resulting partial tree*Tx*induces a partial leaf vector

ˆ*x*:=Leaf*ℓ*(*Tx*)*∈*(*Rq∪{⊥}*) *µ* *.*

Lemma 16(Recursive extraction of a partial digest tree).*Assume Assumption 1. Letdxbe the public root* *digest of a block of widthµ, and letℓ*:= log₂ *µ. Then there is a recursive non-black-box extractor*Exttree *which, given code access to the malicious receiver that produceddx, runs in time at most*

(2 *ℓ* *−*1)*t*dig(*λ*)

*Proof.*A depth-*ℓ*binary tree has at most 2 *ℓ* *−*1 internal nodes, so the recursive procedure invokesExtdigat most that many times. The running-time bound follows immediately. By Assumption 1, each node extraction succeeds and returns the admissible opening except with probability*ϵ*dig(*λ*); a union bound over the calls gives the stated failure probability. Conditioned on this event, the assembled partial tree is consistent at every extracted node, and unresolved branches are exactly the branches on which the recursion stopped.

Role in the malicious proof.Lemma 16 is the knowledge-extraction route for recovering the receiver input without adding the random-linear check. It does not treatDigestas a true random oracle. Rather, it formalizes the narrower knowledge-style property that the protocol needs: if the receiver uses a public digest*dx*in the later translation algebra, then the simulator may recursively extract a partial tree below*dx*, together with explicit bounds on the extractor’s running time and failure probability, and hence obtain the leaf data for every fully recovered branch. To convert the partial leaf vector into an effective receiver input, the simulator fills unresolved leaves with a fixed canonical value, say 0: ( effˆ*xj*if ˆ*xj̸*=*⊥,* *xj*:= 0 if ˆ*xj*=*⊥.*

Outside the extraction-failure event, every leaf that is fixed by an admissible use of the digest has been recovered. The remaining coordinates are not fixed by the digest-knowledge route, so the simulator may submit any valid value for them to*F*CI-VOLE.

### E.5 Malicious security against a corrupted receiver

We now prove the first malicious theorem for the protocol. The proof first replaces the receiver-visible setup packages in the full receiver view, removing the receiver’s useful information about the sender’s hidden ∆-side state. It then follows one of two extraction branches and finally performs a common programming step. For the random-linear-check wrapper, extraction is an active rewinding procedure that varies the check challenge while keeping the online root response fixed. For the digest-knowledge route, extraction invokes the optional knowledge extractor for the public digest. In both cases, once*x* eff is known, the simulator performs one final execution and programs the random-oracle value definingctkso that the corrupted receiver outputs the ideal value.

Ideal functionality.The ideal functionality is the staged CI-VOLE functionality*F*CI-VOLEfrom Figure 6, used with maliciously corrupted parties. For the ring-level protocol we instantiate its parameter*M*as the relevant ring/message space, here*Rq*, and use the instance width*w*. Write the protocol identifier as id= (sid*,*mode), wheremode*∈{*ni*,*int*}*; the mode selects the corresponding setter and release commands of*Figure*6. In the corrupt-receiver case, the simulator must extract an effective receiver input*x* eff *∈R* *w* *q*, submit it usingsetx-niorsetx-int, and obtain the ideal receiver output*m* id *∈R* *w* *q*from the matching mode- specific release point. The simulator does not receive the honest sender’s mask*k*, nor the sender’s correlation input ∆. The simulator then completes the protocol transcript so that the wrapped receiver outputs exactly *m* id.

Imported correctness invariant.The malicious hybrids use one consequence of the semi-honest cor- rectness analysis rather than repeating that analysis. By Lemma 9 and the induction in Section B.3, once the setup objects, root value, and internal correction ciphertexts along a path are fixed, the remaining leaf expand step has the following form. The leaf correction ciphertext enters the raw receiver or sender value through an affine bijection on the correction ciphertext space, and the subsequent denoising, CRT/gadget recombination, aggregation, and trimming are the same deterministic clean-output map used in the semi- honest proof. On the complement of the no-golden correctness bad event for the malicious parameters, this clean-output map is a surjective quotient onto the retained block message space, with a kernel*K*ctthat depends only on the fixed transcript prefix and the block shape. Thus each output block has a fiber that is an affine coset of*K*ct, and a uniform correction ciphertext conditioned on a given clean block output is uniform on that coset. The same statement applies to the sender-side mask computation after fixing the accepted correction path. This imported invariant is the only place where the malicious proof uses the recursive correctness analysis. The receiver-visible setup packages are instances of one setup-package interface. Such a package is a public payloadP= (U₁*,...,*U*t*), for an allowed arity*t∈{*2*,*4*}*, together with a setup proof*π*setupfor the corresponding setup relation. In this protocol the packages are

*Proof of Theorem 11.*The hybrids separate the receiver-only extraction argument from the final joint-output distribution. The target simulator extracts an effective receiver input*x* eff : from the digest itself in the digest-knowledge branch, and from a rewinding experiment in the random-linear-check branch. It sends *x* eff to*F*CI-VOLEand receives the ideal receiver output*m* id. It then runs the honest receiver continuation internally to determine which leaf*H*ctqueries affect the final output and what fixed offset they induce. For each such query, it samples the oracle answer from the appropriate fiber*F* pref *−*1 (*m* id ), so that the honest receiver computation outputs*m* id.

*H₀*: real execution.Hybrid*H₀* is the real malicious execution with corrupted receiver*R* *⋆* and honest sender. The setup packages and setup proofs are real, all random-oracle answers are sampled lazily as uniform strings in their output spaces, and the honest sender output and receiver output are the real protocol outputs.

*H₁*: simulated setup, extraction, and real-output programming.Hybrid*H₁* replaces the receiver- visible setup packages with simulatedLacE*/*LHEpackages and simulated setup proofs. For each package instance, the replacement uses the adaptive simulators from Sections 3.6.2 and 3.6.4:LacE*.*Sim₁ gener- ates the simulatedLacEciphertext state andLacE*.*Sim₂ answers the adaptive evaluation leakages, while LHE*.*Sim₁*,*LHE*.*Sim₂*,*LHE*.*Sim₃ generate the simulated initial ciphertext, adaptive second ciphertexts, and adaptive functional keys. Public non-ciphertext components, such as*B* *∗*, keep their real distribution, and the setup proof is replaced by the simulated proof for the resulting public statement. This is an all-at- once adaptive replacement for each package instance; the finite tuple of package instances is handled by the standard product/hybrid loss over those independent instances. After the setup replacement,*H₁* extracts*x* eff using one of the two branches below. It then completes a sender-facing execution with the real external sender while acting as an honest receiver: it generates a fresh digest*d* *⋆* *x*consistent with*x* eff, using independent digest randomness, and sends*d* *⋆* *x*to the sender through the correct mode-specific interface. This execution fixes a sender-facing label seed*ς* *⋆*, and the simulator computes

the receiver value*m* real from that honest-receiver execution. The malicious receiver and distinguisher are not given*d* *⋆* *x*,*ς* *⋆*, or the sender-facing correction-oracle labels. Instead of answering the designated leaf *H*ctqueries with independent random strings,*H₁* programs them from the regular fibers that force the deterministic honest receiver computation to output*m* real. Thus the auxiliary extraction experiment may omit the honest sender’s output, but the completed*H₁* experiment preserves the joint output distribution seen by a distinguisher.

Digest extraction branch.The digest-knowledge variant performs no rewinding. Once the receiver’s public root digest*dx*is fixed, the simulator applies Lemma 16. The extractor returns a partial digest tree

Lemma 20(Real-output programming preserves oracle distribution).*Conditioned on no*Badpreq*and no* Badseed*⋆, replacing the ordinary leafH*ct*answers by the programmed vectorC* *⋆* *does not change the re-* *ceiver/distinguisher view.*

*Proof.*Fix the receiver transcript before the designated leaf answers and fix*α*pref. Sampling*C* *⋆* uniformly from *F* pref *−*1 (*m* real ) gives a uniform correction vector by the fiber statement in Lemma 18. Thus the programmed answers have exactly the same distribution as ordinary fresh random-oracle answers on all labels visible to the receiver/distinguisher.

Substituting these correction ciphertexts into the deterministic receiver computation makes the wrapped receiver output exactly*m* real. Therefore a distinguisher that receives both parties’ outputs sees the same joint output distribution in*H₀* and*H₁*, except for the setup-replacement, extraction, prequery, and hidden- sender-seed query losses. Hybrid conclusion.The transition*H₀ →H₁* is justified by setup simulation, the inline receiver-transcript setup-replacement lemma for the random-linear-check auxiliary experiment, the digest extractor in the digest- knowledge branch, the random-linear extraction loss in the random-linear-check branch, and regular correc- tion programming with*m* real. This transition is secure for distinguishers that receive the joint sender/receiver output because the sender-side oracle labels used to sample*m* real are hidden from the receiver view, and*H₁* programs the receiver output to the real-protocol value paired with the honest sender output. The transition *H₁ →H₂* replaces that real-protocol output sampling with the identical ideal VOLE output distribution and uses the same regular-correction programming. Therefore the real and ideal receiver views are indistin- guishable up to the setup-simulation, setup-proof simulation, extraction, prequery, hidden-sender-seed query, regular-correction programming, and no-golden correctness losses.

### E.6 Malicious security against a corrupted sender

For a corrupted sender, the setup proofs from Section 10.1.3 certify that the public recursive ciphertext pack- age is explained by one bounded sender state. By the NIZK argument-of-knowledge interface of Section 3.8, the simulator extracts that state. In particular, it extracts the global correlation ∆ eff, the certified recursive

(1)
setup state, and the root state (rrt*,*skr*,*rt).

Theorem 12(Malicious security against a corrupted sender).*Assume the setup proofs of Section 10.1.3* *satisfy the argument-of-knowledge and zero-knowledge properties of Section 3.8, assume the randomized-root* *hiding lemma of Lemma 7 with root min-entropyκ*rt*before the receiver releasesd* *′* rt*, and assume the no-golden* *correctness condition under which the imported semi-honest correctness invariant above holds for arbitrary* *hash-derived correction ciphertexts. The sender supplies the ordinary setup packages, the widened root package, and the accompanying setup proofs. If any proof fails verification, the honest receiver aborts.

The relation*R*setupguarantees that these objects are exactly the objects used by the public ciphertext packages. The simulator now submits *F*CI-VOLE*.*set-delta(S: ∆ eff *,w*)

#### to the ideal functionality.

*H₂*: name the sender-induced root mask.Hybrid*H₂* is distributed identically to*H₁*. Its only purpose is to name the values that will be used in the ideal switch. Let*d* *′* rtdenote the public digest sent by the honest receiver. The proof uses only this public ring element; it is not computed from, opened to, or used to recover the receiver’s private input. *k*rt *′* :=skrt *′* *−*sk *⊤* r*,*rtg *−*1 (*d* *′* rt)*.*

In non-interactive mode, the online random oracle first fixes (skrt *′* *,ς*) at*H*on(id*,d* *′* rt), and the same equation defines*k*rt *′*. This changes no messages and no distributions.

*H*3int: extract the interactive sender mask.This hybrid applies whenmode=int. The simulator maintains the table for*H*ct. If*S* *⋆* queries *H*ct(id*,s,j,i*)

for some*s∈{*0*,*1*}* *λ* before sendingskrt *′* as its online response, the simulator returns a fresh value of the correct ciphertext type and records it at that exact label. After*S* *⋆* sends the online responseskrt *′*, derive *ς*:=*H*seed(id*,*skrt *′* ). The sender-side output is computed using the recorded values at the accepted labels

(id*,ς,j,i*)*.*

For any accepted label that was not queried, the simulator samples the corresponding*H*ctvalue at that time. Queries with other second components are ordinary random-oracle queries and do not affect the mask that the simulator extracts from the acceptedskrt *′* response. The only structural property needed of the internal correction labels is uniqueness: after (id*,ς*) is fixed, every root, internal, and leaf correction point has a single accepted oracle label, and domain separation prevents collisions with other identifiers or modes. If the recursive algorithm reaches the same accepted label twice, both visits use the same recorded oracle answer. After setup extraction and the definition of*k*rt *′*, the sender-side output on each leaf block is a deterministic function of the accepted correction ciphertexts. Concretely, the lower-level expand algorithm computes a raw LHE decryption k ˜ *i* (ctk*ℓ,i*) =LHE*.*Dec(*ki* loc *,*ctk*ℓ,i*)*,*

where*ki* loc is the carried sender key determined by the extracted setup state, the accepted root mask, and the accepted internal correction ciphertexts on the path to leaf*i*. It then applies the same denoising and output post-processing used by the honest algorithm. Let

Post *S* ct: *C*ct*→M* *µ*

be this sender-side post-processing map. By the imported correctness invariant, this is the sender-side instance of the same quotient map after the accepted correction path has been fixed. For each leaf block*i*, the*H*ctvalue at the accepted label therefore determines one mask block

k eff *i*:=Post *S* ctk ˜ *i* (ctk*ℓ,i*)*.*

The concatenation of these blocks is the extracted maskk eff. No oracle programming is needed in this branch. In the ideal hybrid, the simulator submits this mask to the interactive functionality as the corrupted sender’s chosen mask.

*H*3ni: couple the non-interactive sender mask.This hybrid applies whenmode=ni. The non- interactive functionality samples the sender mask itself, so the simulator does not extract a sender-chosen mask in this branch. Instead, it uses the hidden release digest to couple the sender’s later view to the ideal mask. The honest receiver’s idealsetx-nicall is made by the honest receiver, not by the simulator. The simulator does not learn the receiver input or the receiver output. The value it must match is the sender maskksampled by the ideal functionality and later released to the corrupted sender. The relevant random-oracle inputs are controlled by the receiver’s release message*d* *′* rt. Before that message is delivered to*S* *⋆*, the randomized-root hiding lemma and its leftover-hash instantiation make (*B* *∗* *,d* *′* rt) statistically close to a source in which*d* *′* rthas conditional min-entropy at least*κ*rtgiven the sender’s prefix view. Thus a prior query to*H*on(id*,d* *′* rt), or to a derived label *H*ct(id*,d* *′* rt*,ς,j,i*)*,* must guess the receiver’s release digest. If*Q*onand*Q*ctare the sender’s pre-release query counts, and*N*ctis the number of correction labels used by the invocation, then

Pr[Bad *S* preq]*≤*(*Q*on+*Q*ct*N*ct)2 *−κ*rt +*ϵ*root-hide*.*

The simulator aborts into this bad event if such a query occurred. Otherwise, after the idealsetx-nicall has sampled the pending mask and before*d* *′* rtis delivered to*S* *⋆*, the labels that will be used to expose this mask are fresh in the final random-oracle table. This is the same freshness invariant as above, now for the sender’s VOLE component: if a label tied to the released mask was already present, it was a pre-release guess of*d* *′* rt and the execution has already enteredBad *S* preq. The simulator fixes the*H*onanswer as

*H*on(id*,d* *′* rt) = (skrt *′* *,ς*) or*H*on(id*,d* *′* rt) = (skrt *′* *,ς,ρ*chk)

depending on the receiver-extraction variant. It then sets the*H*ctvalues at the labels (id*,d* *′* rt*,ς,j,i*) so that the sender-side expand computation yields the samek. The visible*H*ctanswers are sampled using the same affine-fiber sampler as in the corrupted-receiver proof. After the setup state, root mask, and accepted internal correction ciphertexts are fixed, the leaf correction ciphertext enters the sender’s raw value through an affine bijection, and the imported invariant gives the same fixed-kernel quotient for the clean mask block. Sampling uniformly from the corresponding coset gives exactly the distribution of a uniform random-oracle answer conditioned on the released sender mask. Since the ideal functionality sampleskuniformly, the conditioned oracle answers have the same joint distribution as the real answers, except for the prequery and correctness bad events already named. The induced value*k*rt *′* =skrt *′* *−*sk *⊤* r*,*rtg *−*1 (*d* *′* rt) is the effective non-interactive online mask for this execution.

*H₄*: switch the receiver to the ideal functionality.The honest receiver now interacts with*F*CI-VOLE using its own private input. The simulator does not submit this input and does not learn it. In interactive mode, the simulator submits the extracted maskk eff using

*F*CI-VOLE*.*setk-int(S:sid*,*k eff )*.*

The honest receiver submits its input throughsetx-int, and the simulator invokes

*F*CI-VOLE*.*release-int(S:sid)

to deliver the ideal output to the receiver. In non-interactive mode, the honest receiver’ssetx-nicall has already fixed both its ideal output and the sender mask; when the protocol reaches the release point, the simulator delivers the released mask to*S* *⋆*. By the setup relation, the extracted ∆ eff and recursive state are exactly the state certified by the public setup package. By the online definition of*k*rt *′*, the root relation is satisfied. By the affine leaf equations above and the correctness theorem for the recursive expand computation, the real receiver computation would have produced exactly the same value that*F*CI-VOLEreturns for the receiver’s private input and the extracted sender state. Therefore replacing the real receiver continuation by the ideal receiver continuation is perfect outside denoising failures, the no-golden correctness bad event, andBad *S* preq.

*H₅*: replace the root digest in the sender view.At this point the honest receiver’s output is supplied by the ideal functionality, so the simulator no longer needs a root digest tied to the receiver’s private input. It samples a dummy value *d* b *←R*

and uses *d*brtas the root digest delivered to*S*, with the*H* and*H* labels formed consistently using that value. By the randomized-root hiding lemma of Lemma 7, instantiated through the ring leftover-hash bound of Theorem 4, the real pair (*B,d*) is statistically close to (*B, d*b). This replacement affects only the corrupted sender’s view. The final hybrid is exactly the ideal execution with a corrupted sender. Summing the setup-extraction, root-hiding, non-interactive prequery, random-oracle programming, and correctness losses completes the proof.

### E.7 Putting the Corruption Cases Together

The preceding theorems are stated for one fixed protocol identifierid= (sid*,*mode). With the two corruption cases isolated, we first combine them into a malicious security statement for that fixed identifier. We then lift the statement to executions with many identifiers by a hybrid over identifiers.

Theorem 13(Malicious security for one fixed identifier).*Assuming Theorems 11 and 12, the protocol* *realizesFCI-VOLEwith malicious security for one fixed*id= (sid*,*mode)*.*

*Proof.*The two theorems cover the two possible static corruptions for the fixed identifier. Both simulators use the same ideal functionality syntax from Figure 6: firstset-deltafixes the effective global correlation and width. In non-interactive mode the receiver usessetx-niand laterrelease-ni; in interactive mode the sender usessetk-int, the receiver usessetx-int, and the sender completes the call withrelease-int. If the receiver is corrupted, Theorem 11 extracts the effective receiver input and programs the honest- sender correction values so that the receiver obtains the ideal output. If the sender is corrupted, Theorem 12 extracts the certified sender setup, derives ∆ eff, and aligns the sender mask and honest-receiver output with the ideal functionality. The bad events are disjointly accounted for by the corresponding proof: receiver- input extraction and ∆-hiding losses occur only in the corrupted-receiver case, while setup-proof extraction, no-golden correctness, and sender-side oracle programming losses occur only in the corrupted-sender case. A union bound over the relevant bad events gives the fixed-identifier malicious security statement.

Theorem 14(Full malicious CI-VOLE theorem).*Assume the fixed-identifier theorem from Theorem 13.* *Then the full protocol securely realizesFCI-VOLEwith malicious security for polynomially many identifiers.*

*Proof.*A full execution may contain many identifiers. The setup state and global correlation are fixed once byset-delta. Thereafter, online messages, random-oracle labels, ideal-functionality entries, and release commands are keyed byid. The multi-identifier simulator therefore performs the setup step only once: in the corrupted-receiver case it uses the single simulated setup view for all identifiers, and in the corrupted-sender case it extracts one certified reusable setup witness and submits the corresponding ∆ eff through the one-time setup command *F*CI-VOLE*.*set-delta(S: ∆ eff *,w*)*.*

It also maintains one global random-oracle table.

The hybrids then replace identifiers one at a time while keeping that shared setup and shared oracle table fixed. For the current identifier, the fixed-idtheorem supplies the online transcript replacement and the matching ideal-functionality commands. Oracle entries whose labels contain the currentidare handled by that replacement; entries for other identifiers remain in the global table and are answered exactly as before. Since every online random-oracle label and every functionality-table entry containsid, cross-identifier correlations appear only through the shared setup already handled at the beginning or through collisions in the domain-separated oracle labels. Those collision events are included in the same prequery/collision bad events as above. A union bound over the polynomially many identifiers and their bad events gives the full multi-identifier statement. Finally, the deterministicLogLabelZwrapper applies the label-to-ring and ring-to-label maps from Figure 3, yielding the stated*F*CI-VOLEfunctionality over the top-level message space.

## F Opened ZK Protocol and Proof Sketch

This appendix records the implemented ZK route from Section 11. The verifier uses a fresh semi-honest LogVolesetup for one proof session, and opens that setup after the prover has committed to the values that must be fixed before ∆ is known. This removes the sender-side NIZKs needed by the long-term public-key route, while retaining the optimized golden-seed implementation.

### F.1 Why the Opening Is Sound

The verifier opening serves one purpose: it turns setup well-formedness into a local check by the prover. In the public-key route, the verifier cannot reveal ∆, so the setup must be accompanied by malicious-VOLE certification proofs. In the opened route, the setup is fresh and single use. Once the prover has committed to the random-linear-check answers and the final IT-MAC-check values, the verifier can reveal ∆ and all setup coins. The prover recomputes the prover-visible setup messages and rejects if they do not match. The timing is the essential invariant. The prover sees ∆ only after its accepting proof values are com- mitted. Therefore revealing ∆ lets the prover audit the verifier setup, but it does not let the prover adapt the linear-check answers or the final IT-MAC equation.

### F.2 Proof Sketch

Completeness.For an honest verifier, the opened setup recomputes to the transcript that the prover already used, so the prover’s setup audit accepts. The random-linear-check identities hold by the final LogVoleoutput relation*mi*=*ki*+*xi*∆. The IT-MAC batching equation is the gate identity from Section 11; for a valid trace all gate errors are zero, so the verifier accepts.

Soundness.Fix a cheating prover and condition on a valid verifier opening. If the opening is invalid, the prover rejects and there is no accepting transcript. Before the verifier opens ∆, theLogVoletranscript is fixed and the prover has committed to the random-linear-check answers and the final values (*V*b₁*, V*b₂). By the semi-honest security of the freshLogVolesetup, this pre-opening view hides ∆ except with negligible probability. The random-linear-check adaptor now gives one effective trace. Rewind the prover to the point after its challenge-free transcript is fixed and answer with fresh independent check challenges. Each accepting rewind gives linear equations in the same output vector, with the sacrificed coordinates included as unknowns. Except for the 1*/p*random-hit event per independent field check, enough accepting rewinds determine a unique vectorx eff. This is exactly the extraction role of Section 10.1.6, specialized to the final field-level output used by the ZK application. It remains to check the circuit.
