Thus the verifier’s view is distributed as in a real execution, up to the semi-honestLogVolesimulation and the hiding of the commitments.

Reusable public keys.This proof deliberately uses the verifier opening and is therefore not a proof for a long-lived public key. For that setting, the verifier keeps ∆ hidden and the ZK application should instantiate its label-generation layer with the maliciousCI-VOLEprotocol of Section 10, including the sender-side setup certification proofs.

## G Omitted Details about [AMR25]

Here, we provide a detailed comparison with [AMR25]. We expect that readers have already read our overview (Section 4).

### G.1 Cost Analysis of [AMR25]

[AMR25] reports its communication complexity vaguely aspoly(*λ*)*·*log*|*x*|*in its abstract and does not provide a clear analytic cost for computation. They use different notations for their cost accounting, making a straightforward comparison difficult. Here, we provide a detailed analysis of their analytic cost using our notation. As background, [AMR25] considers a slightly more general problem where two parties want to evaluate the outer productx*·*yfor their respective inputsxandy. The mapping between our notation and [AMR25] is summarized as follows: •Roles: TheScorresponds to the*encoder*; theRcorresponds to the*hasher*. •Artifacts: The*encoding*corresponds to the one-time setup (committing toy); the*digest*corresponds to the per-instance communication (evaluating the product). •Parameters: –*ℓ*=*|*y*|*: Dimension of the vectory. –*m*=*|*x*|*: Dimension of the vectorx. –*r*=*O*(log*|*x*|*): Number of homomorphic hashing levels. –*t >*1: a constant –*n*=*k·*log*q*: Dimension of the public LWE matrix. We also have*n*= Ω(˜ *λ²*) –*k*= Θ(*λ*). –*q*: Modulus for the LWE samples. We also have log*q*= Ω(˜ *λ*) We first focus on the(one-time setup) encoding cost. The protocol is listed asEncodein Construction

3.6 of [AMR25, p. 20]. Its communication, shown in [AMR25, p. 23], involves sending a series of matrices overZ*q*, whose total number of entries is*ℓ·r·t² ·n²*, totaling*ℓ·r·t² ·n² ·*log*q*bits of communication. Here,*ℓ*=*|*y*|*,*r*=*O*(log*|*x*|*) is the number of bootstrap levels, and*t >*1 is a constant. The most significant costs come from*n*and*q*, which require careful analysis.*n*is the number of rows in the public LWE matrix
(A) and*n*=*k·*log*q*[AMR25, p. 20].*k*(*λ*) is set as Θ(*λ*) for security [AMR25, p. 18]. The magnitude of
Ω( ˜ *λ*) *q*is critical, and*q*= 2 for two reasons. First, their construction generally only guarantees approximate correctness,*i.e.*, the results are noisy. If the parties want only negligible errors in the results, they need to lift their messages inZ*p*by*q/p∈λ* *ω*(*λ*) [AMR25, p. 17]. In other words, log*q*= Ω(˜ *λ*). Secondly, for our applications of interest such as ZK and GC, their labels must be from a sufficiently large domain for security. Specifically, we need the message modulus to be*p*= 2 Ω(*λ*). As*p < q*, we also have log*q*= Ω(˜ *λ*). This gives us*n*=*O*(*k*log*q*) = *O*˜(*λ²*). Plugging in these parameters to the encoding size and expressing it in terms of log*|*x*|*and*λ*, we find that the encoding size is*O*(log*|*x*|·k²* log³ *q*) =*O*(log*|*x*|·λ⁵*). For the(per-instance message) digest, the size corresponds to a length-*n*vector of integers, each having a bit-length of*r*log(*tn*) + log*||x||∞*[AMR25, Eq. 6]. Using the parameter conversions above, we have the digest size as *O*˜(*λ²*(log*|*x*|*+*λ*)). Theironline computation costis more implicit as their main focus was showing succinct communica- tion. Instead of inspecting every operation in these protocols, we observe that both protocols need to multiply vectors by a matrix Π *r* *i*=1 *−*1 P*i*, whereP*i*= (I*tr−i ·*P),I*µ*is the identity matrix of dimension*µ×µ*, *n²×*(*t·n²*) 2 andP*∈*Z is a 0*/*1 matrix in which each row has at most*n* non-zero entries [AMR25, p. 21]. Plugging in the parameter *n*= *O*˜(*λ²*), we know multiplying a vector by this matrix would cost *O*˜(*|*x*|·λ⁴*) (potentially large) integer arithmetic operations, and this is the lower bound on computation per instance.

### G.2 Detailed Comparison about Constructions

Readers familiar with [AMR25] might find that both their work and ours exhibit a similar structure: at each level of bootstrap (or recursion), the protocols derive a share of the tensor product (or broadcast multiplication) from the shares of a smaller set of products at the level above. Also, [AMR25] relies on the LWE hardness assumption, while our work assumes hardness of RLWE. Nevertheless, the underlying techniques are vastly different. [AMR25] relies on a theorem in [BLMR13] stating that LWE w.r.t. random matrices,*i.e.*, usingA *T* *·*Bas the LWE matrix whereAandBare random binary matrices, is as hard as standard LWE. This is the key theorem enabling the hasher to reduce the problem size via multiplying its inputs by a fat matrixA, which allows the parties to later expand their shares by multiplying them by a tall matrixB. It is unclear if there is any equivalent structure/theorem in RLWE that enables such a mechanism without introducing new or less-tested hardness assumptions. In contrast, our construction relies on a RLWE-based laconic encryption scheme [DKL + 23a]. Leveraging this scheme requires overcoming a different set of challenges, as highlighted in Section 6.1 and Section 6.2. For example, our construction must remove the noise at every recursion level to maintain correctness, while [AMR25] allows the noise to accumulate across levels.

## H Reported GPT-2 Results for VOLE-based ZK Inference

To provide context for the communication bottleneck in large VOLE-based ML proofs, we collect the GPT-2 inference numbers reported in [LWQ + 24, Section 8.4, Table 5]. Their experiments use a network environment with 500 Mbps bandwidth and about 50 ms latency. We report the prover time, verifier time, proof size, and transfer time claimed in the paper, and additionally list the derived local-generation time, which is the sum of prover and verifier time.
